-\frac{1}{n_{0}T_{0}}\int_{0}^{n_{0}T_{0}}u(\phi^{t}(h(s)))dt\leq l(\pi h[0,s]).

By the argument at the beginning of this section, Green bundles
G^{s/u}
are tangent to the horospherical foliations
{\mathcal{F}}^{s/u}
in our setting. Then we have

\displaystyle l(\pi\phi^{nn_{0}T_{0}}(h[0,a]))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{a}\|d\pi d\phi^{nn_{0}T_{0}}h^{\prime}(s)\|ds=\int_{0}^{a}\|J^{s}_{h(s)}(nn_{0}T_{0})\|ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{a}\|d\pi d\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h^{\prime}(s)\|e^{\int_{0}^{n_{0}T_{0}}u(\phi^{t+(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h(s))dt}ds

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{a}\|d\pi d\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h^{\prime}(s)\|e^{-n_{0}T_{0}l(\pi\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h([0,s]))dt}ds

\displaystyle\geq l(\pi\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}(h[0,a]))e^{-n_{0}T_{0}l(\pi\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h([0,a]))}

\displaystyle\geq l(\pi\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}(h[0,a]))\left(1-n_{0}T_{0}l(\pi\phi^{(n-1)n_{0}T_{0}}h([0,a])\right)

where we used (
10
) and
e^{x}\geq 1+x
for
|x|
small. Denote
a_{n}:=l(\pi\phi^{nn_{0}T_{0}}(h[0,a]))
and
B=n_{0}T_{0}
, we get

(11)

a_{n}\geq a_{n-1}(1-Ba_{n-1})\geq\cdots\geq a_{0}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-Ba_{i}).

Since we assume the contrary that the area of
\pi\phi^{[0,\infty]}h[0,a]
is finite, by bounded asymptote, we have
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}<+\infty
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=0
. Then by (
11
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-Ba_{i})=0

which is equivalent to
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}=+\infty
. We have a contradiction and therefore the theorem follows.
∎

4.3.
Singular geodesics

In this subsection, we discuss some important properties of the singular geodesics. Our Theorem is a straightforward corollary of these properties. In fact, it is closely related to the following two lemmas (Lemma
4.6
and
4.9
).
The first lemma shows that if a flat geodesic converges to a closed one (no matter singular or not), then the former geodesic must also be closed, and coincide with the latter.

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose that
y\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}\cap\text{Per}(\phi^{t})
, and
\lim_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(y),\phi^{t}(z))=0
where
c_{z}(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Then
\mathcal{O}(y)=\mathcal{O}(z)
. In particular,
\mathcal{O}(y)
is bi-asymptotic to an axis of
\gamma
.

Proof.

\tilde{c}_{0}
\tilde{c}
\phi(\tilde{c})
\tilde{\alpha}
\phi(\tilde{\alpha})
\tilde{\beta}
\phi(\tilde{\beta})
A
B
C
D
E
C^{\prime}
D^{\prime}
E^{\prime}
A^{\prime}
B^{\prime}
F

Figure 1.
Proof of Lemma
4.6

We can lift geodesics
c_{z}(t),c_{y}(t)
to the universal cover
X
,
denoted by
\tilde{c}_{1}(t)
and
\tilde{c}(t)
respectively, such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}d(\tilde{c}_{1}(t),\tilde{c}(t))=0.

In particular,
\tilde{c}_{1}(+\infty)=\tilde{c}(+\infty)
.

Since the isometry
\gamma
of
X
fixes
\tilde{c}_{1}(\pm\infty)
, there exists an axis
\tilde{c}_{0}
of
\gamma
such that
\gamma(\tilde{c}_{0}(t))=\tilde{c}_{0}(t+t_{0})
and
\tilde{c}_{0}(\pm\infty)=\tilde{c}_{1}(\pm\infty)
. Moreover, on the boundary of the disk
{\partial X}
,
\gamma
fixes exactly two points
\tilde{c}_{0}(\pm\infty)
, and for any other point
a\in{\partial X}\setminus\{\tilde{c}_{0}(\pm\infty)\}
,
\lim_{n\to+\infty}\gamma^{n}(a)=\tilde{c}_{0}(+\infty)
.

Assume
\tilde{c}(\pm\infty)
is not fixed by
\gamma
. Then
\tilde{c}
and
\gamma(\tilde{c})
do not intersect, since
\gamma(\tilde{c})(+\infty)=\tilde{c}(+\infty)
. Replacing
\gamma
by
\gamma^{N}
for a large enough
N\in\mathbb{N}
if necessary, we know that the position of
\gamma(\tilde{c})
must be as shown in Figure 1. We then pick another two geodesics
\tilde{\alpha}
and
\tilde{\beta}
as in Figure 1. The image of
ABB^{\prime}A^{\prime}
under
\gamma
is
CEE^{\prime}C^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma
is an isometry, it preserves area. So the area of
ABCD
is equal to the region
A^{\prime}B^{\prime}DEE^{\prime}C^{\prime}
, and thus greater than the area of the region
DEE^{\prime}D^{\prime}
. We can let
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
approach
F
. In this process, the area of the region
DEE^{\prime}D^{\prime}
approaches the area of the ideal triangle
DEF
. But since
c_{y}
is a singular and closed geodesic, the area of
DEF
is infinite by Theorem
4.5
. Then
ABCD
has infinite area which is absurd. So
\gamma(\tilde{c}(\pm\infty))=\tilde{c}(\pm\infty)
.

Therefore
\tilde{c}(\pm\infty)=\tilde{c}_{0}(\pm\infty)
. Then either
\tilde{c}(t)
and
\tilde{c}_{0}(t)
bound a strip by Lemma
3.15
or
\tilde{c}(t)=\tilde{c}_{0}(t)
. Recall that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}d(\tilde{c}(t),\tilde{c}_{0}(t))=0
, we must have
\tilde{c}(t)=\tilde{c}_{0}(t)
. Hence
\mathcal{O}(y)=\mathcal{O}(z)
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The above lemma holds more generally without assuming that
y,z
are singular. In fact, this is a rather general property of isometries on
X
. See the proof of Lemma
7.4
. The above proof is of independent interest and uses geometric properties of singular geodesics.

To refine Lemma
4.6
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
M
be a closed surface with genus
\mathfrak{g}\geq 2
, without conjugate points and with bounded asymptote. If
{\underline{w}}
is periodic,
{\underline{w}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}({\underline{w}})\subset SM
and

\liminf_{t\to+\infty}d(c_{\underline{w}}(t),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t))=\delta>0,

then
c_{\underline{w}}(t)
and
c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t)
converge to the boundaries of a nontrivial generalized strip.

Proof.

Suppose that
\lim_{i\to+\infty}d(c_{\underline{w}}(s_{i}),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(s_{i}))=\delta>0
. By passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
\lim_{i\to+\infty}\phi^{s_{i}}({\underline{w}})=v
and
\lim_{s_{i}\to+\infty}\phi^{s_{i}}({\underline{w}}^{\prime})={\underline{v}}^{\prime}
. Then
{\underline{v}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}({\underline{v}})
. Then by Proposition
3.7
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}

d(c_{{\underline{v}}}(t),c_{{\underline{v}}^{\prime}}(t))=\lim_{s_{i}\to+\infty}d(c_{{\underline{w}}}(t+s_{i}),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t+s_{i}))\in[\delta,Q_{1}]

where
Q_{1}:=Ad_{s}(c_{{\underline{v}}}(0),c_{{\underline{v}}^{\prime}}(0))+B
.
Hence we can lift the geodesics to
X
such that
v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
and

d(c_{v}(t),c_{v^{\prime}}(t))\in[\delta,Q_{1}]\text{\ \ \ for\ }\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

By Lemma
3.15
,
c_{v}(t)
and
c_{v^{\prime}}(t)
are the boundaries of a generalized strip of width at least
\delta
.

By assumption,
{\underline{w}}
is periodic and thus
{\underline{v}}\in\mathcal{O}({\underline{w}})
. So there exists
{\underline{v}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}({\underline{w}})
such that
{\underline{v}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}({\underline{v}}^{\prime})
.

(1)

If
{\underline{w}}^{\prime}
is on the arc of
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}({\underline{w}})
between
{\underline{w}}
and
{\underline{v}}_{1}
, then
{\underline{w}}^{\prime}
is inside the generalized strip bounded by geodesics
c_{v}(t)
and
c_{v^{\prime}}(t)
, and we are done.

(2)

If
{\underline{v}}_{1}
is on the arc of
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}({\underline{w}})
between
{\underline{w}}
and
{\underline{w}}^{\prime}
, then:

•

If
\liminf_{t\to+\infty}d(c_{{\underline{v}}_{1}}(t),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t))=0,
we are done since then by bounded asymptote

\lim_{t\to+\infty}d(c_{{\underline{v}}_{1}}(t),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t))=0,

and
{\underline{w}}
and
{\underline{v}}_{1}
are the boundaries of a nontrivial generalized strip.

•

If otherwise
\liminf_{t\to+\infty}d(c_{{\underline{v}}_{1}}(t),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t))>0,
then repeating the above process produces another
{\underline{v}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}({\underline{w}})
. If we are not done in finite steps, since
{\mathcal{I}}({\underline{w}})
has finite length by Proposition
3.7
, we will arrive at a limit point
{\underline{v}}_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{I}}({\underline{w}})
such that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}d(c_{{\underline{v}}_{\infty}}(t),c_{{\underline{w}}^{\prime}}(t))=0.
Then we are done.

∎

Now Lemma
4.6
can be strengthened to the following.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
y\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}\cap\text{Per}(\phi^{t})
and
z\in\omega(y)
where
c_{z}(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Then
\mathcal{O}(y)=\mathcal{O}(z)
. In particular,
\mathcal{O}(y)
is bi-asymptotic to an axis of
\gamma
.

Proof.

Suppose that there exist
s_{k}\to+\infty
such that
\phi^{s_{k}}(y)\to z
. If
\phi^{s_{k}}(y)\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(z)
for some
k
then we must have
\lim_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t+s_{k}}(y),\phi^{t}(z))=0
by Lemma
4.8
and its proof. Then by Lemma
4.6
, we have
\mathcal{O}(\phi^{s_{k}}(y))=\mathcal{O}(z)
. So we are done.

Suppose that
\phi^{s_{k}}(y)\notin{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(z)
for any
k
. Note that if
y\neq z
then
y
and
z
can not be tangent to a same generalized strip. Therefore by Lemma
4.4
, for any small
\epsilon_{0}>0
and any large
k
, there exists a
l_{k}
with
l_{k}\to+\infty
such that

d(\phi^{l_{k}}(\phi^{s_{k}}(y)),\phi^{l_{k}}(z))=\epsilon_{0}\text{\ \ or\ \ }d(\phi^{l_{k}}(-\phi^{s_{k}}(y)),\phi^{l_{k}}(-z))=\epsilon_{0}

where we take
l_{k}
to be the smallest positive number to satisfy the above equality. We only consider the first case. By taking a subsequence, we can assume that

\phi^{l_{k}}(\phi^{s_{k}}(y))\to y^{+}\text{\ \ and \ \ }\phi^{l_{k}}(z)\to z^{+}

as
k\to+\infty
. Then
z^{+}
is such that
c_{z^{+}}(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
and
d(y^{+},z^{+})=\epsilon_{0}
.

We claim that in the above process we can make sure that the footpoint of
y^{+}
is outside of the generalized strip bounded by
c_{\gamma}
and
c_{z}
, where
c_{\gamma}
is an axis (fixed once for all) of
\gamma
. Indeed, we can take
\phi^{s_{k}}(y)
so that the footpoint of
\phi^{l_{k}}(\phi^{s_{k}}(y))
is outside of the above generalized strip. If this is not the case, we then consider
-\phi^{s_{k}}(y)
and
-z
instead. Under this change, we still get a limit point
y^{+}
and
z^{+}
, so that
y^{+}
is outside of the generalized strip bounded by
c_{\gamma}
and
c_{z}
.

Then for any
t>0
, since
0<-t+l_{k}<l_{k}
for large enough
k
, one has

d(\phi^{-t}(y^{+}),\phi^{-t}(z^{+}))=\lim_{k\to+\infty}d(\phi^{-t+l_{k}+s_{k}}(y)),\phi^{-t+l_{k}}(z))\leq\epsilon_{0}.

So
-y^{+}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(-z^{+})
. Then we have the following two cases:

(1)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-y^{+}),\phi^{t}(-z^{+}))=0
. Then by bounded asymptote,
\lim_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-y^{+}),\phi^{t}(-z^{+}))=0
. By Lemma
4.6
,
c_{y^{+}}(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
and in fact
-y^{+}=-z^{+}
. This contradicts to
d(y^{+},z^{+})=\epsilon_{0}
.

(2)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-y^{+}),\phi^{t}(-z^{+}))=\delta
for some
\delta>0
. By Lemma
4.8
,
\phi^{t}(-y^{+})
converges to the boundary of a generalized strip containing
-z^{+}
. Then by Lemma
4.6

c_{y^{+}}
and
c_{z^{+}}
are boundaries of a generalized strip of width at least
\delta
. Recall that
y^{+}
is outside of the generalized strip bounded by
c_{\gamma}
and
c_{z}
which implies that we have enlarged the generalized strip bounded by
c_{\gamma}
and
c_{z}
to the one bounded by
c_{\gamma}
and
c_{y^{+}}
.
Now note that
y^{+}\in\omega(y)
, so we can apply all the arguments above to
y^{+}
instead of
z
. Either we are arriving at a contradiction as in case (1) and we are done, or we get an even larger generalized strip. Since
{\mathcal{I}}(\dot{c}_{\gamma})
has finite length by Propostion
3.7
, at last we will get a limit vector
y_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{I}}(\dot{c}_{\gamma})
such that
y_{\infty}\in\omega(y)
if we are always in case (2). Then we can apply all the arguments above to
y_{\infty}
and we must arrive at case (1). So we are done.

∎

Theorem 4.10
.

If
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}\subset\text{Per}(\phi^{t})
, then there is a finite decomposition of
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
:

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}\cup\ldots\mathcal{O}_{k}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{S}_{l},

where each
\mathcal{O}_{i},1\leq i\leq k
, is an isolated periodic orbit and each
\mathcal{S}_{j},1\leq j\leq l
, consists of vectors tangent to a generalized strip. Here
k
or
l
are allowed to be
0
if there is no isolated closed singular geodesic or no generalized strip.

Proof.

Assume the contrary to Theorem
4.10
. Then there exists a sequence of different vectors
x^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
such that
\lim_{k\to+\infty}x^{\prime}_{k}=x
for some
x\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
. Here different
x^{\prime}_{k}
are tangent to different closed geodesics or to different generalized strips, and
x
is tangent to a closed geodesic or to a generalized strip. For large enough
k
, we suppose that
d(x^{\prime}_{k},x)<\frac{1}{k}
.

Fix a small number
\epsilon_{0}>0
. It is impossible that
d(\phi^{t}(x^{\prime}_{k}),\phi^{t}(x))\leq\epsilon_{0}
,
\forall t>0
. For otherwise,
c_{x^{\prime}_{k}}(t)
and
c_{x}(t)
are positively asymptotic closed singular geodesics. They must be tangent to a common generalized strip by Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.6
. This is impossible since different
x^{\prime}_{k}
are tangent to different closed geodesics or to different flat strips. Hence there exists a sequence
s_{k}\to+\infty
such that

d(g^{s_{k}}(x^{\prime}_{k}),g^{s_{k}}(x))=\epsilon_{0},

and

d(g^{s}(x^{\prime}_{k}),g^{s}(x))\leq\epsilon_{0},\ \forall 0\leq s<s_{k}.

Let
y_{k}:=g^{s_{k}}(x)
and
y^{\prime}_{k}:=g^{s_{k}}(x^{\prime}_{k})
. Without loss of generality, we suppose that
y_{k}\to a
and
y^{\prime}_{k}\to b
.
Then
d(a,b)=\lim_{k_{i}\to+\infty}d(g^{s_{k_{i}}}(x_{k_{i}}),g^{s_{k_{i}}}(x^{\prime}_{k_{i}}))=\epsilon_{0}.
We get that
d(a,b)=\epsilon_{0}
.
For any
t<0
, since
0<s_{k_{i}}+t<s_{k_{i}}
for some large
k_{i}
, one has

d(\phi^{t}(a),\phi^{t}(b))=\lim_{i\to+\infty}(d(g^{s_{k_{i}}+t}(x_{k_{i}}),g^{s_{k_{i}}+t}(x^{\prime}_{k_{i}})))\leq\epsilon_{0}.

Hence we get that
d(\phi^{t}(a),\phi^{t}(b))\leq\epsilon_{0}
,
\forall t\leq 0
.

Replacing
x,x_{k}^{\prime}
by
-x,-x_{k}^{\prime}
respectively and applying the same argument, we can obtain two points
a^{-},b^{-}
such that
d(a^{-},b^{-})=\epsilon_{0}
and

d(\phi^{t}(a^{-}),\phi^{t}(b^{-}))\leq\epsilon_{0},\quad\forall t\leq 0.

Then we have the following three cases:

(1)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-a),\phi^{t}(-b))=0
. By bounded asymptote, this is equivalent to
\lim_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-a),\phi^{t}(-b))=0
. By Lemma
4.6
,
-a=-b
. This contradicts to
d(a,b)=\epsilon_{0}
.

(2)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-a^{-}),\phi^{t}(-b^{-}))=0
. Again by Lemma
4.6
,
-a^{-}=-b^{-}
. This contradicts to
d(a^{-},b^{-})=\epsilon_{0}
.

(3)

\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-a),\phi^{t}(-b))>0
and
\liminf_{t\to\infty}d(\phi^{t}(-a^{-}),\phi^{t}(-b^{-}))>0
.

For case (3), by Lemmas
4.8
and
4.9
,
c_{a}
and
c_{x}
coincide. And moreover,
c_{x}
and
c_{b}
are boundaries of a generalized strip of width
\delta_{1}>0
. Similarly,
c_{x}
and
c_{b^{-}}
are boundaries of a generalized strip of width
\delta_{2}>0
. We claim that these two generalized strips lie on the different sides of
c_{x}
. Indeed, we choose
\epsilon_{0}
small enough and consider the
\epsilon_{0}
-neighborhood of the closed geodesic
c_{x}
which contains two regions lying on the different sides of
c_{x}
. By the definition of
b
and
b^{-}
, they must lie in different regions as above. This implies the claim.

In this way we get a generalized strip of width
\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
and
x
is tangent to the interior of this generalized strip. Since
g^{s_{k}}(x^{\prime}_{k})\to b
, we can repeat all the arguments above to
b,g^{s_{k}}(x^{\prime}_{k})
instead of
x,x_{k}^{\prime}
. Then either we are arriving at a contradiction as in case (1) or case (2) and we are done, or we get a generalized strip of width greater than
\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}
and
b
is tangent to the interior of the generalized strip. Proceeding in this way, either we arrive at case (1) or case (2) in finite steps, or at last we will get a limit generalized strip which cannot be enlarged by Proposition
3.16
. Applying the above argument to this limit generalized strip, we have to arrive at case (1) or case (2) and thus obtain a contradiction. So we are done with the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A
.

By the argument at the beginning of this section, the geodesic flow has a periodic hyperbolic point. Then by Theorem
3.4
,
\Delta
agrees
\nu
-almost everywhere with the open dense set
\mathcal{R}_{1}
. This proves the first statement in Theorem
A
. Then Theorem
A
(1) follows from Theorem
4.5
, and Theorem
A
(2) follows from Theorem
4.10
.
∎

5.
Patterson-Sullivan measure and Bowen-Margulis measure

5.1.
Patterson-Sullivan measure

In this subsection we assume that
X
is a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate
points. We will recall the construction of the Patterson-Sullivan measure presented in
[
78
]
and obtain some properties, especially the shadowing lemma.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and
\Gamma
a discrete subgroup of
\text{Iso}(X)
, the group of isometries of
X
. For a given constant
r>0
, a family of finite Borel measures
\{\mu_{p}\}_{p\in X}

on
{\partial X}
is called an
r
-dimensional
Busemann density
if

(1)

for any
p,\ q\in X
and
\mu_{p}
-a.e.
\xi\in{\partial X}
,

\frac{d\mu_{q}}{d\mu_{p}}(\xi)=e^{-r\cdot b_{\xi}(q,p)}

where
b_{\xi}(q,p)
is the Busemann function;

(2)

\{\mu_{p}\}_{p\in X}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, i.e., for all Borel sets
A\subset{\partial X}
and for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we have

\mu_{\gamma p}(\gamma A)=\mu_{p}(A).

In
[
78
]
, the authors constructed Busemann density via Patterson-Sullivan construction. For each pair of points
(p,p_{0})\in X\times X
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

Poincaré series
is defined as

P(s,p,p_{0}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}e^{-s\cdot d(p,\alpha p_{0})}.

This series may diverge for some
s>0
. The number

\delta_{\Gamma}:=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}\mid P(s,p,p_{0})<+\infty\}

is called the
critical exponent of
\Gamma
. By the triangle inequality, it is easy to check that
\delta_{\Gamma}
is independent of
p
and
p_{0}
. We say
\Gamma
is of
divergent type
if the Poincaré series diverges when
s=\delta_{\Gamma}
.
Whether
\Gamma
is of divergent type or not is independent of the choices of
p
and
p_{0}
.

Fix a point
p_{0}\in X
. For each
s>\delta_{\Gamma}
and
p\in X
, consider the measure:

\mu_{p,s}:=\frac{\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}e^{-s\cdot d(p,\alpha p_{0})}\delta_{\alpha p_{0}}}{\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}e^{-s\cdot d(p_{0},\alpha p_{0})}},

where
\delta_{\alpha p_{0}}
is the Dirac measure at point
\alpha p_{0}
. The following properties of
\mu_{p,s}
can be easily checked by using the triangle inequality:

(1)

e^{-s\cdot d(p,p_{0})}\leq\mu_{p,s}(\overline{X})\leq e^{s\cdot d(p,p_{0})}
.

(2)

\Gamma p_{0}\subset\text{supp}\mu_{p,s}\subset\overline{\Gamma p_{0}}
, where
\Gamma p_{0}
is the orbit of
p_{0}\in X
under the action of
\Gamma
.

Here in constructing a Busemann density, we consider the case that the group
\Gamma
is of divergent type first.
When
\Gamma
is of convergent type, Patterson showed in
[
90
]
that one can weight the Poincaré series with a positive monotone increasing function to make the refined Poincaré series diverge at
s=\delta_{\Gamma}
.
More precisely, we consider a positive monotonely increasing function
g:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
with

\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{g(x+t)}{g(t)}=1
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
such that the weighted Poincaré series

\widetilde{P}(s,p,p_{0}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}g(d(p,\alpha p_{0}))e^{-s\cdot d(p,\alpha p_{0})}

diverges at
s=\delta_{\Gamma}
. For the construction of the function
g
, see
[
90
, Lemma 3.1]
. Then for the weighted Poincaré series
\widetilde{P}(s,p,p_{0})
,
we can always construct a Busemann density by using the same method as follows.

Now for
p\in X
, consider a weak
⋆
limit

\lim_{s_{k}\downarrow h}\mu_{p,s_{k}}=\mu_{p}.

Since
P(s,p_{0},p_{0})
is divergent for
s=\delta_{\Gamma}
and
\Gamma
is discrete, it is obvious that
\text{supp}(\mu_{p})\subset\overline{\Gamma p_{0}}\cap{\partial X}
.

Proposition 5.2
.

There is a sequence
s_{k}\to\delta_{\Gamma}
as
k\to\infty
such that for every
p\in X
the weak
∗
limit
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{p,s_{k}}=:\mu_{p}
exists. Moreover,
\{\mu_{p}\}_{p\in X}
is a
\delta_{\Gamma}
-dimensional Buseman density.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
[
30
, Proposition 5.1]
. Note that we do not need to assume that
X
admits a compact quotient.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The uniform visibility condition is essential in the above construction. For example, under that condition,
\partial X
with cone topology is well defined and the continuity of Busemann functions is guaranteed.

5.2.
Bowen-Margulis measure

Let
P:SX\to{\partial X}\times{\partial X}
be the projection given by
P(v)=(v^{-},v^{+})
. Denote by
\mathcal{I}^{P}:=P(SX)=\{P(v):v\in SX\}
the subset of pairs in
{\partial X}\times{\partial X}

which can be connected by a geodesic. Note that the connecting geodesic may not be unique. Since
X
has uniform visibility, every pair
\xi\neq\eta
in
{\partial X}
can be connected by a geodesic, i.e.,

\partial^{2}X=({\partial X}\times{\partial X})\setminus\{(\xi,\xi):\xi\in{\partial X}\}.

Fix a point
p\in X
, we can define a
\Gamma
-invariant measure
\overline{\mu}

on
\partial^{2}X
by the following formula:

(12)

d\overline{\mu}(\xi,\eta):=e^{\delta_{\Gamma}\cdot\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)}d\mu_{p}(\xi)d\mu_{p}(\eta),

where
\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta):=-\{b_{\xi}(q,p)+b_{\eta}(q,p)\}
is the
Gromov product
, and
q
is any point on a geodesic
c
connecting
\xi
and
\eta
. It is easy to see that the function
\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)
does not depend on the choice of
c
and
q
.
In geometric language, the Gromov product
\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)
is the length of the part of a geodesic

c
between the horospheres
H_{\xi}(p)
and
H_{\eta}(p)
.

Let
M=X/\Gamma
. Define an equivalence relation on
SM
by writing
v\sim w
iff
w\in{\mathcal{I}}(v)
.
Write
[v]={\mathcal{I}}(v)
for the equivalence class of
v
. Let
\tilde{\chi}:SX\to SX/\sim
and
\chi:SM\to SM/\sim
be the quotient maps.
These are continuous maps when we equip the quotient spaces with the metric

d([v],[w])=\min\{d(v^{\prime},w^{\prime}):v^{\prime}\in[v],w^{\prime}\in[w]\}.

The geodesic flow
\phi^{t}
takes equivalence classes to equivalence classes,
\phi^{t}[v]=[\phi^{t}v]
, hence it induces a flow
\psi^{t}:SM/\sim\to SM/\sim
.

Theorem 5.4
.

(
[
81
, Theorem 1.1]
)
Let
M
be a compact
C^{\infty}

n
-dimensional manifold without conjugate points and with uniform visibility universal cover
X
. Then the geodesic flow
\phi^{t}
is time-preserving semi-conjugate to the continuous flow
\psi^{t}
acting on a compact metric space
SM/\sim
of topological dimension at least
n-1
. Moreover,
\psi^{t}
is expansive, topologically mixing and has a local product structure.

We do not need compactness of
M
in the following construction.
Since
{\mathcal{I}}(v)
is compact and
SM
is a Polish space, the measurable selection theorem of Kuratowski and Ryll-Nardzewski
[
111
, Section 5.2]
guarantees existence of a Borel measurable map
V:SM/\sim\to SM
such that
\chi\circ V
is the identity. Recall that
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}:SX\to SM
is the natural projection. Then for every
v\in SX
,
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}^{-1}(V(\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}[v]))
intersects
[v]
in a single point, which we denote by
\tilde{V}([v])
. We conclude that
\tilde{V}:SX/\sim\to SX
is a measurable map such that
\tilde{\chi}\circ\tilde{V}
is the identity, and moreover
\tilde{V}(\gamma_{*}[v])=\gamma_{*}\tilde{V}([v])
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Now define a measure
\lambda_{\xi,\eta}
on each
P^{-1}(\xi,\eta)
by fixing any
v\in P^{-1}(\xi,\eta)
and putting for
a Borel measurable set
A\subset SX

\lambda_{\xi,\eta}(A):=\text{Leb}\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\tilde{V}([\phi^{t}v])\in A\}.

Note that this is independent of the choice of
v
. Use this to define a measure
m
on
SX
by

m(A)=\int_{\partial^{2}X}\lambda_{\xi,\eta}(A)d\overline{\mu}(\xi,\eta).

Observe that
m
is
\Gamma
-invariant, and it descends to a Borel measure
m_{\Gamma}
on
SM
.

The measure
m_{\Gamma}
is not necessarily invariant under the geodesic flow, since along a flow orbit inside a generalized strip, the choice for the value of measurable function
V
may vary. However, the measure
{\underline{m}}_{\Gamma}=\chi_{*}m_{\Gamma}
is a flow invariant measure on
SM/\sim
because
\chi_{*}\lambda_{\xi,\eta}
is flow invariant on each
P^{-1}(\xi,\eta)/\sim
.

Now let us suppose that
M
is compact and then clearly
m_{\Gamma}
is finite. Without loss of generality we scale the measure so that
m_{\Gamma}(SM)=1
. Then the set
\chi_{*}^{-1}{\underline{m}}_{\Gamma}
of Borel probability measures is weak
∗
compact and closed under
(\phi^{t})_{*}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. So the usual argument from the Krylov-Bogolyubov theorem for producing an invariant probability measure shows that there is a flow invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
on
SM
with
\chi_{*}\mu={\underline{m}}_{\Gamma}
. This lifts to a
\Gamma
-invariant and flow invariant Borel measure
\tilde{\mu}
on
SX
by

\tilde{\mu}(A)=\int_{SM}\#(\text{pr}^{-1}({\underline{v}})\cap A)d\mu({\underline{v}}).

We will call both
m_{\Gamma}
and
\mu

Bowen-Margulis measures
of the geodesic flow on
SM
.

Remark 5.5
.

The above construction of
\mu
for a compact manifold
M
without conjugate points which satisfies the first two conditions in Definition
2.7
of class
\mathcal{H}
has appeared in
[
30
]
. In the last section, we will prove the Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy for the geodesic flow on a uniform visibility (not necessarily compact) manifold without conjugate points with respect to the Bowen-Margulis measure
m_{\Gamma}
.

5.3.
Relation to the MME

In this subsection, we always assume that
M=X/\Gamma
is a compact uniform visibility manifold with universal cover
X
. We shall show that the Bowen-Margulis measure
\mu
coincides with the unique MME under the setting of Theorem
2.8
.

By Freire and Mañé’s Theorem (cf.
[
45
]
), the topological entropy of the geodesic flow coincides with the volume entropy, i.e., for any
x\in X

h_{\text{top}}(\phi^{1})=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\text{Vol}B(x,r)}{r}

where
B(x,r)
is the open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
in
X
. Denote
h=h_{\text{top}}(\phi^{1})
. By the proof of
[
79
, Lemma 3.3]
, we have
\delta_{\Gamma}=h
where
\delta_{\Gamma}
is the critical exponent of
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
p\in X
,
\emph{\text{supp}}(\mu_{p})={\partial X}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\text{supp}(\mu_{p})\neq{\partial X}
, then there exists
\xi\in{\partial X}
which is not in
\text{supp}(\mu_{p})
. Then we can find an open neighborhood
U
of
\xi
in
{\partial X}
such that
\mu_{p}(U)=0
.
Since
M
is compact, the geodesic flow is topologically transitive and hence the action of
\Gamma
on
{\partial X}
is minimal (cf.
[
38
, Proposition 3.6 and the proof of Proposition 2.8]
). Then
\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Gamma}\alpha U={\partial X}
. So for any
\eta\in{\partial X}
, there is an element
\alpha\in\Gamma
such that
\eta\in\alpha U
. By the
\Gamma
-invariance
\mu_{\alpha p}(\alpha U)=\mu_{p}(U)=0
. Since
\mu_{p}
is equivalent to
\mu_{\alpha p}
, we have
\mu_{p}(\alpha U)=0
. Therefore
\text{supp}(\mu_{p})=\emptyset
, a contradiction.
∎

For each
\xi\in{\partial X}
and
p\in X
, define the projections

•

pr_{\xi}:X\rightarrow{\partial X},~~q\mapsto c_{\xi,q}(+\infty)
,

•

pr_{p}:X\backslash\{p\}\rightarrow{\partial X},~~q\mapsto c_{p,q}(+\infty)
,

where
c_{\xi,q}
is the geodesic satisfying
c_{\xi,q}(-\infty)=\xi
and
c_{\xi,q}(0)=q
. For
A\subset X
and
p\in X
,
pr_{p}(A)=\{pr_{p}(q)\mid q\in A\backslash\{p\}\}.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists
R>0
such that for all
x\in\overline{X}
and
p\in X
, the shadow
pr_{x}B(p,R)
contains an open set in
{\partial X}
, where
B(p,R)
is the open ball of radius
R
centered at
p
.

Proof.

For
x\in\overline{X}
and
p\in X
, let
v=-\dot{c}_{p,x}(0)
. For small
\rho>0
, the set
A(v,\rho):=\{c_{w}(\infty):\angle_{p}(v,w)<\rho\}
is open in
{\partial X}
. Since
X
is a uniform visibility manifold, for any
\eta\in A(v,\rho)
, there exists at least one geodesic
c_{x,\eta}
connecting
x
and
\eta
. For any
0<\epsilon<\pi-\rho
, let
R=R(\epsilon)
be the constant in Definition
2.1
of uniform visibility manifolds. Assume that the geodesic
c_{x,\eta}
does not intersect the ball
B(p,R)
, then
\angle_{p}(-v,\dot{c}_{p,\eta}(0))<\epsilon
. Therefore,

\angle_{p}(v,-v)\leq\angle_{p}(v,\dot{c}_{p,\eta}(0))+\angle_{p}(-v,\dot{c}_{p,\eta}(0))<\rho+\epsilon<\pi

which is a contradiction. Thus
A(v,\rho)\subset pr_{x}B(p,R)
and the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold which has a compact quotient, and
\{\mu_{p}\}_{p\in X}
be an
\delta_{\Gamma}
-dimensional Busemann density on
{\partial X}
. Fix
r>R
where
R
is from Lemma
5.7
.

(1)

There exists
l=l(r)
such that
\mu_{p}(pr_{x}B(p,r))\geq l
, for all
p\in X
and
x\in\overline{X}
;

(2)

there is a positive constant
a=a(r)
such that for any
x\in X\setminus\{p\}
and
\xi=c_{p,x}(-\infty)
,
we have

\frac{1}{a}e^{-hd(p,x)}\leq\mu_{p}(pr_{\xi}B(x,r))\leq ae^{-hd(p,x)};

(3)

there is a positive constant
b=b(r)
such that for any
p,x\in X
,

\frac{1}{b}e^{-hd(p,x)}\leq\mu_{p}(pr_{p}B(x,r))\leq be^{-hd(p,x)}.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
[
30
, Proposition 5.4]
, for which Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
are crucial. We have established these two lemmas based on the uniform visibility and the compactness of
M
.
∎

Let
\mu
be the Bowen-Margulis measure of the geodesic flow on
SM
, and
d_{1}
be the Knieper metric on
SM
defined as

d_{1}({\underline{v}},{\underline{w}}):=\max_{t\in[0,1]}d(c_{\underline{v}}(t),c_{\underline{w}}(t)).

Consider a finite measurable partition
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{1},...,A_{l}\}
of
SM

satisfying
\mu(\partial A_{i})=0,i=1,\cdots l
and that the radius of every
A_{i}
under
d_{1}
is less than
\epsilon<\min\{\text{Inj}(M),R\}
,
where
R>0
is the constant in Lemma
5.7
.
Let
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\phi}:=\vee^{n-1}_{i=0}\phi^{-i}\mathcal{A}
,
n=1,2,\cdots
.
For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\phi}
, we know that if
{\underline{v}}\in\alpha
, then

\alpha\subset\bigcap^{n-1}_{k=0}\phi^{-k}B_{d_{1}}(\phi^{k}{\underline{v}},\epsilon),

where
B_{d_{1}}(\phi^{k}{\underline{v}},\epsilon)
denotes the
\epsilon
-ball of
\phi^{k}{\underline{v}}\in SM
under the Knieper metric
d_{1}
.
If
{\underline{w}}\in\alpha
, then
d(c_{\underline{v}}(t),c_{\underline{w}}(t))\leq\epsilon
for all
0\leq t\leq n
.

Proposition 5.9
.

There exists a constant
a>0
such that for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{n}_{\phi}
, we have
\mu(\alpha)\leq e^{-hn}a
. As a consequence,
\mu
is a MME.

Proof.

Step 1.
Let
p\in X
be the reference point used in the definition of
\bar{\mu}
in (
12
), and let
v
be a lift of
{\underline{v}}
such that
d(p,\pi{\underline{v}})\leq\text{diam}M=:r_{0}
. Since
\text{diam}\alpha<\text{Inj}(M)
, we can lift
\alpha
to
\tilde{\alpha}
in
X
such that for any
v,w\in\tilde{\alpha}
, then
d(c_{v}(t),c_{w}(t))\leq\epsilon
for all
0\leq t\leq n
. Let
c_{v}(n)=x
, then there exists constant
r_{1}>0
,

P(\tilde{\alpha})\subset\bigcup_{\eta\in pr_{x}(B(p,r_{1}))}\{\eta\}\times pr_{\eta}(B(x,\epsilon)).

Indeed, let
\eta=c_{w}(-\infty)
and
c_{\eta,x}
the geodesic connecting
\eta
and
x
such that
c_{\eta,x}(n)=x
. Since
c_{\eta,x}
and
c_{w}
are negatively asymptotic, by Proposition
3.7
,

d(c_{\eta,x}(t),c_{w}(t))\leq Ad_{s}(c_{\eta,x}(n),c_{w}(n))+B\leq 2A\epsilon+B=:R_{1}

for any
0\leq t\leq n
.
Then
d(c_{\eta,x}(0),p)\leq d(c_{\eta,x}(0),\pi w)+d(\pi w,p)\leq R_{1}+r_{0}+\epsilon=:r_{1}.

This implies that
\eta\in pr_{x}(B(p,r_{1}))
.

Step 2.

\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq e^{-hn}a
for some constant
a>0
.

Indeed, for any
\eta\in pr_{x}(B(p,r_{1}))
, choose a point
q\in B(p,r_{1})
that lies on
c_{\eta,x}
.
First notice that

d(q,x)\geq d(x,\pi v)-d(\pi v,p)-d(p,q)\geq n-r_{0}-r_{1}.

Then by Proposition
5.8
(2),

(13)

\mu_{p}(pr_{\eta}(B(x,\epsilon)))\leq e^{hd(p,q)}\mu_{q}(pr_{\eta}(B(x,\epsilon)))\leq e^{hr_{1}}ae^{-hd(q,x)}\leq\tilde{b}e^{-hn}.

From definition we have
\lambda_{\xi,\eta}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq\text{diam}\tilde{\alpha}
. Recall that
\text{diam}\tilde{\alpha}\leq\epsilon
. We have

m_{\Gamma}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq\bar{\mu}(P(\tilde{\alpha}))\text{diam}\tilde{\alpha}

and the same holds for
(\phi^{t})_{*}m_{\Gamma}
. Since
\tilde{\mu}
is a limit of a convex combination of such measures, we also have

(14)

\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq\bar{\mu}(P(\tilde{\alpha}))\text{diam}\tilde{\alpha}.

At last, notice that for any
(\xi,\eta)\in P(\tilde{\alpha})
,
\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)\leq 2r_{1}
since any geodesic connecting
\xi
and
\eta
intersects
B(p,r_{1})
. Combining this with (
13
), (
14
) and the fact that
\mu_{p}(pr_{x}((B(p,r_{1})))\leq\mu_{p}({\partial X})
, we get
\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq e^{-hn}a
for some constant
a>0
.

Step 3.
We have

H_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\phi})=-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\phi}}\mu(\alpha)\log\mu(\alpha)\geq n\cdot h-\log a,

which implies that

h_{\mu}(\phi^{1})\geq h_{\mu}(\phi^{1},\mathcal{A})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\phi})\geq h.

Then we have
h_{\mu}(\phi^{1})=h
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold which has a compact quotient
M
. Suppose that the geodesic flow on
SM
has a unique MME giving full weight to
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Then for any
A\subset SX
,

(15)

\tilde{\mu}(A)=\int_{\partial^{2}X}\lambda_{\xi,\eta}(A)d\bar{\mu}(\xi,\eta)

where
\lambda_{\xi,\eta}
is the Lebesgue measure on
P^{-1}(\xi,\eta)
which is a single geodesic for
\bar{\mu}\text{-a.e.}\ (\xi,\eta)
.

Proof.

We claim that
P^{-1}(\xi,\eta)
is a single geodesic for
\bar{\mu}\text{-a.e.}\ (\xi,\eta)
. Indeed, if otherwise we must have
\tilde{\mu}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c})>0
, a contradiction. Then the right side of (
15
) immediately gives an invariant measure and indeed it coincides with
\tilde{\mu}
.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold which has a compact quotient
M
. Suppose that the geodesic flow on
SM
has a unique MME giving full weight to
\mathcal{R}_{0}
. Then the MME is fully supported on
SM
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
\tilde{\mu}(U)>0
for any open subset
U\subset SX
. Recall that the Patterson-Sullivan measures have full support on
{\partial X}
and thus
\bar{\mu}(P(U))>0
. Then by (
15
),
\tilde{\mu}(U)>0
. The proposition follows.
∎

The above proposition proves the first statement of Theorem
B
.

Remark 5.12
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
5.10
, we see that
\mu
and
m_{\Gamma}
defined before Remark
5.5
represents the same measure. In the following, we use
m_{\Gamma}
or just
m
to denote this measure, i.e., the Bowen-Margulis measure and the unique MME, since the notation
\mu
usually denotes an abstract measure.

5.4.
Bernoulli property of Bowen-Margulis measure

We prove Theorem
B
in this subsection. To start, we recall some necessary definitions.

Definition 5.13

(Kolmogorov property)
.

Let
T:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. We say that
T
has the
Kolmogorov property
, or that
T
is
Kolmogorov
, if there is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{B}
which satisfies

(1)

\mathcal{K}\subset T\mathcal{K}
,

(2)

\bigvee_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{i}\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{B}
,

(3)

\bigcap_{i=0}^{\infty}T^{-i}\mathcal{K}=\{\emptyset,Y\}
.

Consider
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a probability vector
p=(p_{0},\cdots,p_{k-1})
, i.e.,
p_{i}\geq 0,i=0,\cdots,k-1
and
\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}p_{i}=1
. A
Bernoulli shift
is a transformation
\sigma
on the space
\Sigma_{k}:=\{0,\cdots,k-1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
given by

\sigma((x_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}})=(x_{n+1})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
which preserves the Bernoulli measure
p^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
on
\Sigma_{k}
.

Definition 5.14

(Bernoulli property)
.

Let
T:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. We say that
T
has the
Bernoulli property
or is called
Bernoulli
if it is measurably isomorphic to a Bernoulli shift.

Obviously, the Bernoulli property implies the Kolmogorov property.

Definition 5.15
.

A measure-preserving flow
(Y,(f^{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}},\mu)
is said to have the
Kolmogorov (resp. Bernoulli) property
if for every
t\neq 0
, the invertible measure-preserving transformation
(Y,f^{t},\mu)
has the Kolmogorov (resp. Bernoulli) property.

For the the geodesic flow on manifolds of nonpositive curvature, Call and Thompson
[
26
]
showed that the unique equilibrium state for certain potential has the Kolmogorov property and the unique MME has the Bernoulli property, based on the earlier work of
[
70
]
. These two results are extended to manifolds without focal points in
[
28
, Theorem B]
and
[
120
, Theorem C]
respectively, via methods developed in
[
26
]
. Recently, Araujo-Lima-Poletti
[
4
]
shows that the unique equilibrium state for certain potential has the Bernoulli property by symbolic approach.

5.4.1.
Kolmogorov property

For compact uniform visibility manifolds without conjugate points, we first show the following.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed
C^{\infty}
visibility manifold without conjugate points. Suppose that

\sup\{h_{\mu}(\phi^{1}):\mu\text{\ is supported on\ }\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}\}<h_{{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm top}}}}(\phi^{1}).

Then
m
(i.e., the unique MME) is Kolmogorov.

We first recall Ledrappier’s criterion for Kolmogorov property of equilibrium states
[
70
]
.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
f:Y\to Y
be a continuous self-map on a compact metric space
(Y,d)
. For
\varepsilon>0
and
x\in Y
, denote

\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(x):=\{y\in Y:d(f^{n}(x),f^{n}(y))<\varepsilon,\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}\},

and

h_{\text{loc}}(f,\varepsilon):=\sup\{h_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm top}}}(f,\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(x))):\ x\in Y\}.

We say that
(Y,f)
is
entropy expansive
if there is
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

h_{\text{loc}}(f,\varepsilon_{0})=0,

and
(Y,f)
is
asymptotically entropy expansive
if

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}h_{\text{loc}}(f,\varepsilon)=0
.

We say that
\nu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{f}(Y)
is
almost entropy expansive
at scale
\varepsilon_{0}>0
if

h_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm top}}}(f,\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(x)))=0

for
\nu\text{-a.e.}\ x\in Y
.

Theorem 5.18

(Ledrappier
[
70
]
, Call-Thompson
[
26
]
)
.

Let
(Y,f^{t})
be a continuous flow on a compact metric space such that
f^{t}
is asymptotically entropy expansive for all
t\neq 0
, and let
\psi\in C(Y,{\mathbb{R}}
). Consider
(Y\times Y,f^{t}\times f^{t})
which is the product of two copies of
(Y,f^{t})
, and
\Psi(x_{1},x_{2}):=\psi(x_{1})+\psi(x_{2})
. If
\Psi
has a unique equilibrium state in
\mathcal{M}_{f^{t}\times f^{t}}(Y\times Y)
, then the unique equilibrium state for
\psi
in
\mathcal{M}_{f^{t}}(Y)
has the Kolmogorov property.

We state again Theorem
5.4
here, since we will consider the Kolmogorov property of the expansive factor of the geodesic flow.

Theorem 5.19
.

(Mamani-Ruggiero
[
81
, Theorem 1.1]
)
Let
M
be a compact
C^{\infty}
manifold of dimension
n
without conjugate points and with uniform visibility universal cover
X
. Then the geodesic flow is time-preserving semi-conjugate to a continuous expansive flow
\psi^{t}
acting on a compact metric space
Y:=SM/\sim
of topological dimension at least
n-1
. Moreover,
\psi^{t}
is topologically mixing and has a local product structure.

Denote
P(t,\epsilon)
the set of periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}
of
(Y,\psi^{t})
with period in
[T-\epsilon,T+\epsilon]
. Define the measure

\nu_{t}:=\frac{1}{\#P(t,\epsilon)}\sum_{{\mathcal{O}}\in P(t,\epsilon)}\delta_{\mathcal{O}}

where
\delta_{\mathcal{O}}
is an invariant probability measure supported on the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
. If
\nu=\lim_{t\to\infty}\nu_{t}
, we say that
\nu
is the limit of the invariant measures supported by periodic orbits of
\psi^{t}
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
\nu
be the unique MME of
(Y,\psi^{t})
. Then
\nu\times\nu
is the unique MME of
(Y\times Y,\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t})
. Moreover,
\nu\times\nu
is the limit of the invariant measures supported by periodic orbits of
\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
.

Proof.

Since
(Y,\psi^{t})
has the specification property, by
[
26
, Lemma 3.1]
,
(Y\times Y,\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t})
has the specification property as well. However,
(Y\times Y,\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t})
is not necessarily expansive even if
(Y,\psi^{t})

is expansive. We overcome this difficulty following ideas of
[
26
]
.

Denote
\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x):=\{y\in Y:d(\psi^{t}x,\psi^{t}y)<\epsilon,\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
and

\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x,y):=\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in Y\times Y:d(\psi^{t}x,\psi^{t}y),d(\psi^{t}x^{\prime},\psi^{t}y^{\prime})<\epsilon,\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

Note that
\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x,y)=\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x)\times\Gamma_{\epsilon}(y)
. The set of
non-expansive points
at scale
\epsilon>0
is defined as

\text{NE}(\epsilon):=\{x\in Y:\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x)\nsubseteq\psi_{[-s,s]}(x),\forall s>0\}.

According to
[
26
, Definition 4.2]
, the set of
product non-expansive points
at scale
\epsilon>0
is denoted by

\text{NE}^{\times}(\epsilon):=\{(x,y)\in Y\times Y:\Gamma_{\epsilon}(x,y)\nsubseteq\psi_{[-s,s]}(x)\times\psi_{[-s,s]}(y),\forall s>0\}.

By
[
26
, Lemma 4.4]
,

\text{NE}^{\times}(\epsilon)\subset(Y\times\text{NE}(\epsilon))\cup(\text{NE}(\epsilon)\times Y).

Since
\psi^{t}
is expansive,
\text{NE}(\epsilon)=\emptyset
and hence
\text{NE}^{\times}(\epsilon)=\emptyset
. Thus any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}}(Y\times Y)
is product expansive at any scale
\epsilon
, in the sense that
\mu(\text{NE}^{\times}(\epsilon))=0
.
It follows from
[
26
, Proposition 4.7]
that every subset
Q\subset Y\times Y
invariant under the
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
-action (i.e.,
\{\psi^{s}\times\psi^{t}):s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
) can be approximated by elements of adapted partitions for
(t,\epsilon)
-separated sets.

Now we recall some results obtained from Climenhaga-Thompson’s proof
[
32
]
of uniqueness of MME for
\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
. In fact, even Bowen’s original proof (cf.
[
14
]
or
[
63
, Theorem 20.1.3]
) is applicable to our setting. We can obtain similarly as
[
26
, Theorem 6.3 and Lemma 6.4]
:

•

\nu
is weakly mixing, which is equivalent to that
\nu\times\nu
is ergodic;

•

\nu\times\nu
has the Gibbs property, i.e. there exists
Q>0
such that for any
(x,y)\in Y\times Y
and
t>0
,

(\nu\times\nu)(B_{t}((x,y),\rho))\geq Q^{2}e^{-2th}

where
B_{t}((x,y),\rho)
is the Bowen ball and
h
is the topological entropy of
\psi^{t}
(and hence of
\phi^{t}
).

Combining these facts, we can adapt the classical arguments to subset
Q\subset Y\times Y
invariant under the
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
-action. The proof is a verbatim of
[
26
, page 818]
. Moreover,
\nu\times\nu
is the limit of the invariant measures supported by periodic orbits of
\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
(cf.
[
14
]
or
[
17
, Proposition 6.4]
).
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

(
[
24
, Theorem 1.5]
)
Let
f^{t}:Y\to Y
and
g^{t}:Z\to Z
be two continuous flows on compact metric spaces,
\tilde{\chi}:Y\to Z
be a time-preserving semiconjugacy and
\nu
be the unique MME of
g^{t}
. Assume that
g^{t}
is expansive, has the specification property and

(1)

h(f^{1},\tilde{\chi}^{-1}(x))=0
for every
x\in Z
;

(2)

\nu(\{\tilde{\chi}(y):\tilde{\chi}^{-1}(\tilde{\chi}(y))=\{y\}\})=1
.

Then
f^{t}
has a unique MME.

Proof of Proposition
5.16
.

We apply Lemma
5.21
for
f^{t}=\phi^{t}\times\phi^{t}
and
g^{t}=\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
and
\tilde{\chi}(x,y)=(\chi(x),\chi(y))
where
\chi:SM\to SM/\sim
is the projection. All the conditions of Lemma
5.21
can be verified except that
\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
is expansive. However, if we look at the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 1.5]
, we see that we do not need the expansive condition once we know that
\nu\times\nu
is the limit of the invariant measures supported by periodic orbits of
\psi^{t}\times\psi^{t}
.

By
[
23
]
, any
C^{\infty}
dynamical system is asymptoically
h
-expansive. So the geodesic flow
\phi^{t}
is asymptoically
h
-expansive. By Lerappier’s criterion Theorem
5.18
, we see that
m
has the Kolmogorov property.
∎

5.4.2.
From Kolmogorov to Bernoulli

A classical argument in
[
88
]
showed that the Kolmogorov property implies Bernoulli property for smooth invariant measures of Anosov flows. The argument was also carried out by Chernov and Haskell
[
29
]
for smooth invariant measures of suspension flows over some nonuniformly hyperbolic maps with singularities. It also works for hyperbolic invariant measures with local product structure. See also
[
98
,
97
]
for an introduction to terminology and ideas of the Kolmogorov-Bernoulli equivalence.

Based on the argument in Chernov and Haskell
[
29
]
, Call and Thompson
[
26
, Section
7
]
proved that the Knieper measure is Bernoulli when
M
is a rank one compact manifold of nonpositive curvature. Following
[
29
,
26
]
, we show that the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
is Bernoulli for compact uniform visibility manifolds without conjugate points in the setting of Theorem
2.8
.

Let
f:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. If
A\subseteq Y
is a measurable subset, then by the measure
\mu
on
A
we mean the conditional measure
\mu|_{A}(B):=\frac{\mu(A\cap B)}{\mu(A)}
. By
\alpha|_{A}
we mean the induced partition on the space
(A,\mu|_{A})
, i.e.,
\alpha|_{A}:=\{A\cap A_{1},\cdots,A\cap A_{k}\}.

Definition 5.22
.

Let
\alpha
be a finite measurable partition of
Y
and
\varepsilon>0
. We say that a property holds for
\varepsilon
-almost every element of
\alpha
if the union of those elements for which the property fails has
\mu
-measure at most
\varepsilon
.

Definition 5.23
.

Let
f:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. A finite partition
\alpha
is called a
very weak Bernoulli partition
(VWB partition) if for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
N^{\prime}\geq N\geq N_{0}
,
S\geq 1
, and
\varepsilon
-almost every element
A
of
\vee_{N}^{N^{\prime}}f^{k}\alpha
we have

\bar{d}((f^{-i}\alpha)_{i=0}^{S-1},(f^{-i}\alpha|_{A})_{i=0}^{S-1})\leq\varepsilon

where
\bar{d}
is a distance between two sequences of partitions (cf.
[
98
, Definition 3.4]
).

Theorem 5.24

(Cf.
[
86
,
87
]
)
.

Let
f:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
. If there exists a sequence of VWB partitions
\alpha_{k}
converging to the partition into points of
Y
, then
f
is Bernoulli.

We continue to recall the classical method in
[
88
]
and
[
29
]
to establish VWB property for systems with hyperbolic behaviour. Given a finite partition
\alpha=\{A_{1},\cdots,A_{k}\}
, the
\alpha
-name of
x\in Y
is the sequence
(x_{i}^{\alpha})
determined by
f^{i}(x)\in A_{x_{i}^{\alpha}}
. A measurable map
\theta:(Y,\mu)\to(Z,\nu)
is called
\varepsilon
-measure preserving
if there exists a subset
E\subset Y
with
\mu(E)<\varepsilon
such that for every
A\subset Y\setminus E
, one has

\left|\frac{\nu(\theta(A))}{\mu(A)}-1\right|<\varepsilon.

Lemma 5.25

(Cf.
[
88
]
)
.

Let
f:Y\to Y
be an invertible measure-preserving transformation in a Lebesgue space
(Y,\mathcal{B},\mu)
and
\alpha
a finite measurable partition of
Y
. If for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
N^{\prime}\geq N
,
S\geq 1
, and
\varepsilon
-almost every atom
A
of
\vee_{N}^{N^{\prime}}f^{k}\alpha
one can find a map
\theta:(A,\mu|_{A})\to(Y,\mu)
such that

(1)

there is a subset
E^{\prime}\subset A
with
\mu|_{A}(E^{\prime})<\varepsilon
such that for any
x\in A\setminus E^{\prime}
,

h(x,\theta(x)):=\frac{1}{S}\sum_{0\leq i\leq S-1:x_{i}^{\alpha}\neq(\theta(x))_{i}^{\alpha}}1<\varepsilon,

(2)

\theta
is
\varepsilon
-measure preserving,

then
\alpha
is a VWB partition.

Now let
m
be the Bowen-Margulis measure, which coincides with the the unique MME for the geodesic flow in Theorem
2.8
. We will lift the Kolmogorov property of
m
to the Bernoulli property, and thus finish the proof of Theorem
B
.
Since
(Y,\psi^{t})
has local product structure, we will prove that
\nu
has the Bernoulli property. Since
m(\mathcal{R}_{0})=1
, we see that
\chi:(SM,m)\to(Y,\nu)
is a measure-preserving isomorphism. Then we can conclude that
m
has the Bernoulli property. On the other hand, we will make use of the product structure of
m
as in (
15
) in the proof.

Consider a partition
\alpha=\{A_{1},\cdots,A_{k}\}
into subsets of
Y
with arbitrarily small diameter and the following property: there exists some
D>0
such that
\nu(\partial_{\varepsilon}A_{i})\leq D\varepsilon,i=1,\cdots,k
for any
\varepsilon>0
where
\partial_{\varepsilon}A_{i}:=\{y\in M:d(y,\partial A_{i})<\varepsilon\}
. The existence of such a partition with respect to
m
on compact manifold
SM
is guaranteed by
[
88
, Lemma 4.1 and (4.3)]
. Then we project this partition onto
Y
to get partition
\alpha
we need. It is enough to show that any above partition
\alpha
is VWB. Therefore, the task is to construct the function
\theta
in Lemma
5.25
for such
\alpha
. To do it, we localize the problem and construct
\theta
in small rectangles defined as follows.

5.4.3.
\varepsilon
-regular coverings

Since
(Y,\psi^{t})
has local product structure, we construct rectangles first in
Y
, and then lift them to
SM
. We note that even near expansive vectors, there is no well defined local product structure on
SM
. One may assume the continuity of Green bundles so that there is a local product structure near a vector in the open set
\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Here we do not assume this condition, but use the local product structure of
Y
and local product structure of
m
on
SM
provided by (
15
).

For every
v\in SM
, we define
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}(v):=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\phi^{t}({\mathcal{F}}^{s/u}(v))
and

(16)

V^{*}([v]):=\chi({\mathcal{F}}^{*}(v)),*=s,u,0s,0u.

For
[v],[w]
close enough, define
[[v],[w]]
to be the single point in

V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}([v])\cap V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}([w])=\chi({\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(v)\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(w)).

Definition 5.26
.

A
rectangle
in
Y
is a measurable set
R\subset Y
, equipped with a distinguished point
z\in R
with the property that for all points
x,y\in R
the local weak stable manifold
V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(x)
and the local unstable
V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(y)
intersect each other at a single point in
R
, denoted by
[x,y]
.

Recall that
m(\mathcal{R}_{0})=\nu(\chi(\mathcal{R}_{0}))=1
. Take any
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, and let

R:=[V_{\epsilon}^{0s}([v_{0}]),V_{\epsilon}^{u}([v_{0}])]

for some small
\epsilon>0
. Then
R
is a rectangle in
Y
centered at
[v_{0}]
since
Y
has local product structure.
Since
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, we can take
\epsilon>0
small enough, so that
\tilde{R}=\chi^{-1}(R)
has small enough diameter.

Notice that a rectangle
R
centered at
z\in Y
can be thought of as the Cartesian product of
V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(z)\cap R
and
V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(z)\cap R
, where a point
y\in R
is given by
[V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(y)\cap V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(z),V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(y)\cap V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(z)]
.

Given a probability measure
\nu
on
Y
, there is an associated natural product measure

\nu^{p}_{R}:=\nu^{u}_{z}\times\tilde{\nu}^{0s}_{z},

where
\nu^{u}_{z}
is the conditional measure induced by
\nu
on
V_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(z)\cap R
with respect to the measurable partition of
R
into local unstable manifolds, and
\tilde{\nu}^{0s}_{z}
is the corresponding factor measure on
V^{0s}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}(z)
.

Below is Chernov and Haskell’s definition of
\epsilon
-regular coverings.

Definition 5.27
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
, we define an
\epsilon
-regular covering
for a probability measure
\nu
of
Y
to be a finite collection of disjoint rectangles
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}
such that

(1)

\nu(\cup_{R\in\mathcal{R}}R)>1-\epsilon
;

(2)

For every
R\in\mathcal{R}
with a distinguished point
z\in R
, the product measure
\nu^{p}_{R}:=\nu^{u}_{z}\times\tilde{\nu}^{0s}_{z}
satisfies

\left|\frac{\nu^{p}_{R}(R)}{\nu(R)}-1\right|<\epsilon.

Moreover,
R
contains a subset
G
with
\nu(G)>(1-\epsilon)\nu(R)
such that for all
x\in G
,

\left|\frac{d\nu^{p}_{R}}{d\nu}(x)-1\right|<\epsilon.

Lemma 5.28
.

For any
\delta>0
and
\epsilon>0
, there exists an
\epsilon
-regular covering of connected rectangles
\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}
for the measure
\nu
of
Y
, with
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm diam}}}(R)<\delta
for any
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Since
(Y,\psi^{t})
has local product structure, we can find a finite
collection of disjoint rectangles
R
constructed as above, covering a subset of
\nu
-measure at least
1-\epsilon
and satisfying the first condition in Definition
5.27
. Moreover, the rectangles
R
can be chosen such that
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm diam}}}R<\delta
. Let
\tilde{R}=\chi^{-1}(R)
. Since
R
is centered at expansive vectors, the diameter of
\tilde{R}
is also small.

To verify the second condition in Definition
5.27
, we use the local product structure of
m
provided by (
15
). We lift all objects to the universal cover
X
. Take a lift of
\tilde{R}
, which is still denoted by
\tilde{R}
. Since
\tilde{R}
is centered at some
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, the local weak horospherical stable
and local horospherical unstable manifolds of
v_{0}
intersect at a single point
v_{0}
. For any
x\in\tilde{R}
, there exists a continuous map
\varphi_{x}:{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)\to{\partial X}
given by
\varphi_{x}(v)=v^{+}
where
v\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)
. By (
15
), the conditional measure
\mu^{u}_{x}
on
\tilde{R}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)
of the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
is given by

(17)

d\mu^{u}_{x}(v)=\frac{e^{h\beta(x^{-},\varphi_{x}v)}d\mu_{p}(\varphi_{x}v)}{\int_{\tilde{R}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)}e^{h\beta(x^{-},\varphi_{x}v)}d\mu_{p}(\varphi_{x}v)}

and
\int dm(v)=c\int d\mu^{u}_{x}(v)d\mu_{p}(x^{-})dt
for some normalization constant
c>0
.

Given two points
x,y\in\tilde{R}
, the local weak stable holonomy map
\pi^{0s}_{xy}:{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)\cap\tilde{R}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(y)\cap\tilde{R}
is defined by

\pi_{xy}^{0s}(w):=[w,y]={\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(y)\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{0s}(w)\cap\tilde{R},\quad w\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)\cap\tilde{R}.

Note that
\varphi_{x}(w)=\varphi_{y}(\pi^{0s}_{xy}w):=\eta_{w}
. By (
17
), the Jacobian of the holonomy map

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d(\pi_{yx}^{0s})_{*}\mu^{u}_{y}}{d\mu^{u}_{x}}(w)\right|=\left|\frac{e^{h\beta(y^{-},\eta_{w})}}{e^{h\beta(x^{-},\eta_{w})}}\cdot\frac{\int_{\tilde{R}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)}e^{h\beta(x^{-},\eta_{w})}d\mu_{p}(\eta_{w})}{\int_{\tilde{R}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}^{u}(x)}e^{h\beta(y^{-},\eta_{w})}d\mu_{p}(\eta_{w})}\right|.

By Corollary
3.12
,
e^{h\beta}
is uniformly continuous and bounded away from
0
. By taking
\delta
small enough so that
\text{diam\ }\tilde{R}
is small, we have that

\left|\frac{d(\pi_{yx}^{0s})_{*}\mu^{u}_{y}}{d\mu^{u}_{x}}(w)-1\right|<\epsilon

for
x,y\in\tilde{R}
.
By definition of conditional measures, this implies the second condition in Definition
5.27
for
m
.

Since
\chi:(\tilde{R},m)\to(R,\nu)
is an isomorphism, and
\chi
preserves foliations in the sense of (
16
), the second condition in Definition
5.27
for
\nu
also holds.
∎

5.4.4.
Construction of the function
\theta

To conclude of the proof of Theorem
B
, we need construct the function
\theta
in Lemma
5.25
, for any partition
\alpha=\{A_{1},\cdots,A_{k}\}
into subsets of
X
with
\nu(\partial_{\varepsilon}A_{i})\leq D\varepsilon,i=1,\cdots,k
for some
D>0
and any
\varepsilon>0
. The existence of
\varepsilon
-coverings by Lemma
5.28
allows us to construct the function
\theta
by first constructing it in a small rectangle.

Lemma 5.29
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exists
0<\delta_{1}<\delta
such that if
R
is a rectangle with
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm diam}}}R<\delta_{1}
and
E
is a measurable set intersecting
R
leafwise, i.e.,
V^{u}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}(x)\cap R\subset E\cap R
for any
x\in E\cap R
, we can construct a bijective function
\theta:E\cap R\to R
such that for any measurable set
F\subset E\cap R
,

(18)

\frac{\nu^{p}_{R}(\theta(F))}{\nu^{p}_{R}(R)}=\frac{\nu^{p}_{R}(F)}{\nu^{p}_{R}(E\cap R)}

and
\theta(x)\in V^{cs}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}(x)
for every
x\in E\cap R
.

Proof.

We remark that according to the disintegration

\int dm(v)=c\int d\mu^{u}_{x}(v)d\mu_{p}(x^{-})dt,

the measure
d\mu_{p}(x^{-})dt
has no atom, and the same holds for
\nu
. Then the construction of
\theta
in the lemma is to match the points in two local unstable sets by weak stable holonomy, see
[
96
, Lemma 5.10 ]
or
[
97
, Lemma 4.9]
for the smooth measure.
∎

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Based on (
18
) and the definition of
\varepsilon
-regular covering, we can prove item (2) in Lemma
5.25
.

Since
\theta(x)\in V^{0s}_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm loc}}}(x)
for every
x\in E\cap R
, we have by
[
81
, Proposition 5.1]
that for any
\epsilon>0
, if we take
\delta>0
at the beginning of the construction of
R
small enough, then

d(\psi^{t}(x),\psi^{t}(\theta(x)))\leq\epsilon

for any
t>0
.
Item (1) in Lemma
5.25
follows from the above fact
and condition on the small boundary of
\alpha
with respect to
\nu
.

Since the diameter of
\alpha
can be arbitrarily small, by Theorem
5.24
,
\nu
is Bernoulli. Since
\chi:(SM,m)\to(Y,\nu)
is a measure-preserving isomorphism,
m
is Bernoulli, which completes the proof of Theorem
B
.
∎

6.
Uniqueness of Equilibrium states

In this section, we prove Theorem
C
using the symbolic approach developed in Lima-Poletti
[
72
]
. We first prepare the general theorem on coding of nonuniformly hyperbolic flows. Then we apply the nonuniform hyperbolicity of the geodesic flow on uniform visibility manifolds without conjugate points, and with continuous Green bundles.

6.1.
Coding of nonuniformly hyperbolic flows

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(V,E)
be an oriented graph, where
V,E
are the vertex and edge sets. Denote edges by
v\to w
, and assume that
V
is countable. Suppose that
{\mathcal{G}}
is locally compact, namely, for all
v\in V
, the number of ingoing edges
u\to v
and outgoing edges
v\to w
is finite.

A
topological Markov shift
(TMS for short) is a pair
(\Sigma,\sigma)
where

\Sigma:=\{{\underline{v}}=\{v_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in V^{{\mathbb{Z}}}:v_{n}\to v_{n+1},\forall n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}

is the symbolic space endowed with the metric

d({\underline{v}},{\underline{w}}):=\exp(-\inf\{|n|\in{\mathbb{Z}}:v_{n}\neq w_{n}\}),

and
\sigma:\Sigma\to\Sigma
is the left shift defined by
[\sigma({\underline{v}})]_{n}=v_{n+1}
. The
regular set
of
\Sigma
is defined as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\#}:=\{{\underline{v}}\in\Sigma:\exists u,w\in V\text{\ such that\ }

\displaystyle v_{n}=u\text{\ for infinitely many\ }n>0,

\displaystyle\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle v_{n}=w\text{\ for infinitely many\ }n<0\}.

Let
(\Sigma,\sigma)
be a TMS and
r:\Sigma\to(0,+\infty)
be a continuous function. For
n\geq 0
, the
n
-th Birkhoff sum of
r
is

r_{n}:=r+r\circ\sigma+\cdots+r\circ\sigma^{n-1}.

The definition can be extended for
n<0
in a unique way such that the following cocycle identity holds:

r_{m+n}=r_{m}+r_{n}\circ\sigma^{m},\quad\forall m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Definition 6.1
.

The
topological Markov flow
(TMF for short) is the pair
(\Sigma_{r},\sigma_{r})
where

\Sigma_{r}:=\{({\underline{v}},t):{\underline{v}}\in\Sigma,0\leq t<r({\underline{v}})\}

and
\sigma_{r}:\Sigma_{r}\to\Sigma_{r}
is a flow on
\Sigma_{r}
defined by

\sigma_{r}^{t}({\underline{v}},t^{\prime})=(\sigma^{n}({\underline{v}}),t^{\prime}+t-r_{n}({\underline{v}}))

where
n
is the unique integer such that
r_{n}({\underline{v}})\leq t^{\prime}+t<r_{n+1}({\underline{v}})
.

\Sigma_{r}
can be endowed with a natural metric
d_{r}(\cdot,\cdot)
, the so-called Bowen-Walters metric, so that
\sigma_{r}
is a continuous flow. The
regular set
of
(\Sigma_{r},\sigma_{r})
is
\Sigma_{r}^{\#}:=\{({\underline{v}},t)\in\Sigma_{r}:{\underline{v}}\in\Sigma^{\#}\}
.

If
\Sigma
is a TMS defined by an oriented graph
{\mathcal{G}}=(V,E)
, its
irreducible components
are the subshifts
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
defined over maximal subsets
V^{\prime}\subset V
satisfying the following condition:

\forall u,w\in V^{\prime},\exists{\underline{v}}\in\Sigma\text{\ and\ }n\geq 1\text{\ such that\ }v_{0}=u\text{\ and\ }v_{n}=w.

An
irreducible component

\Sigma^{\prime}_{r}
of
\Sigma_{r}
is a set of the form

\Sigma^{\prime}_{r}=\{({\underline{v}},t)\in\Sigma_{r}:{\underline{v}}\in\Sigma^{\prime}\}

where
\Sigma^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
\Sigma
.

Now we consider a
C^{1+\beta}(\beta>0)
-vector field
Z
with
Z\neq 0
everywhere on a compact smooth manifold
N
. Let
\varphi^{t}:N\to N

be the flow generated by
Z
. We say that
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\varphi^{t}}(N)
is
\lambda
-hyperbolic
(
\lambda>0
), if for
\mu\text{-a.e.}\ x\in N
, all the Lyapunov exponents are greater than
\lambda
in absolute value, except for the zero exponent in the flow direction. By Pesin theory, for
\mu\text{-a.e.}\ x\in N
, there exists
stable/unstable manifold
through
x

W^{s/u}(x):=\{y\in N:\limsup_{t\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log d(\varphi^{t}(x),\varphi^{t}(y))<0\}.

The
weak stable/unstable manifold
through
x
is then defined as

W^{0s/0u}(x):=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\varphi^{t}(W^{s/u}(x)).

Definition 6.2
.

We say that two ergodic hyperbolic measures
\mu,\nu
are
homoclinically related
if for
\mu\text{-a.e.}\ x
and
\nu\text{-a.e.}\ y
there exist transverse intersections
W^{0s}(x)\pitchfork W^{0u}(y)\neq\emptyset
and
W^{0u}(x)\pitchfork W^{0s}(y)\neq\emptyset
, i.e., points
z_{1}\in W^{0s}(x)\cap W^{0u}(y)
and
z_{2}\in W^{0u}(x)\cap W^{0s}(y)
such that
T_{z_{1}}N=T_{z_{1}}W^{0s}(x)+T_{z_{2}}W^{0u}(y)
and
T_{z_{2}}N=T_{z_{1}}W^{0u}(x)+T_{z_{2}}W^{0s}(y)
.

Theorem 6.3
.

(
[
72
, Theorem 2.1]
)
Let
\varphi^{t}:N\to N
be a flow generated by a
C^{1+\beta}
-vector field
Z
with
Z\neq 0
everywhere, and
\lambda>0
. If
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
are homoclinically related
\lambda
-hyperbolic ergodic measures, then there is an irreducible TMF
(\Sigma_{r},\sigma_{r})
and a Hölder continuous map
\pi_{r}:\Sigma_{r}\to N
such that:

(1)

r:\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is Hölder continuous and bounded away from zero and infinity.

(2)

\pi_{r}\circ\sigma_{r}^{t}=\varphi^{t}\circ\pi_{r}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(3)

\pi_{r}[\Sigma_{r}^{\#}]
has full measure with respect to
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.

(4)

Every
x\in N
has finitely many preimages in
\Sigma_{r}^{\#}
.

6.2.
Uniqueness of equilibrium states

Let
(M,g)
be a closed
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundles, and let
\psi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
be Hölder continuous or of the form
\psi=q\psi^{u}
for
q\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point
{\mathcal{O}}
. We let
W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})=W^{0s/0u}(x)
denote the weak stable/unstable manifold of
{\mathcal{O}}
, for any
x\in{\mathcal{O}}
. The
homoclinic class
of a hyperbolic periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
is the set

\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})=\overline{W^{0u}({\mathcal{O}})\pitchfork W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})}.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}}):=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\phi^{t}({\mathcal{F}}^{s/u}(x))
for any
x\in{\mathcal{O}}
. Then

W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}}).

Proof.

We first show that
W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})
. Recall that for hyperbolic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
,

(19)

W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})=\cup_{t\geq 0}\phi^{-t}(W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}}))

where

W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})=\{y\in SM:d(\phi^{t}(y),{\mathcal{O}})<\epsilon,\forall t>0\}

for any
\epsilon>0
. Then for any
y\in W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})
,
y
is asymptotic to
{\mathcal{O}}
, and therefore
y\in{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. So
W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. By the
\phi^{t}
-invariance of
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
, we have
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
.

Note that both
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
are connected smooth submanifold of dimension
\dim M
. To prove
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
, it is enough to prove that
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
are both open and closed in the induced topology of
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. Since we have proved that
W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
for any
\epsilon>0
, it is easy to see from Proposition
3.7
that
W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})
is open in
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. By (
19
),
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
is open in
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. To prove that it is also closed in
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
, pick a sequence
z_{k}\in W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
such that
z_{k}\to z
as
k\to\infty
for some
z\in{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. Then

d(\phi^{t}(z),{\mathcal{O}})\leq d(\phi^{t}(z),\phi^{t}(z_{k}))+d(\phi^{t}(z_{k}),\phi^{t}({\mathcal{O}}))\leq Cd(z,z_{k})+d(\phi^{t}(z_{k}),{\mathcal{O}}).

Choose
k
large enough such that
d(z_{k},z)<\epsilon/2C
and
t_{0}
large enough such that
d(\phi^{t}(z_{k}),{\mathcal{O}})\leq\epsilon/2
for any
t\geq t_{0}
. Therefore,
d(\phi^{t}(z),{\mathcal{O}})\leq\epsilon
for any
t\geq t_{0}
. We get
\phi^{t_{0}}(z)\subset W^{0s}_{\epsilon}({\mathcal{O}})
and thus
z\in W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
. This proves
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})
.

The proof for
W^{0u}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0u}({\mathcal{O}})
is analogous and we are done.
∎

By Proposition
3.7
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}/{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
are continuous minimal foliations of
SM
. Hence
W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})
are dense in
SM
.

Lemma 6.5
.

\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})=SM
.

Proof.

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
U\subset\mathcal{R}_{1}
an open neighborhood of
y
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is open, we have
B_{\epsilon}(y)\subset U\subset\mathcal{R}_{1}
for small enough
\epsilon>0
. By Lemma
6.4
,
W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})
are dense in
SM
. Hence there exist
z_{1}\in W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})\cap B_{\epsilon}(y)
and
z_{2}\in W^{0u}({\mathcal{O}})\cap B_{\epsilon}(y)
.

By Proposition
3.7
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(z_{1})
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(z_{2})
are submanifolds tangent to
G^{s}(z_{1})\oplus\langle Z(z_{1})\rangle
and
G^{u}(z_{2})\oplus\langle Z(z_{2})\rangle
respectively. Since
G^{s}
and
G^{u}
vary continuously, and
G^{s}(y)\oplus\langle Z(y)\rangle\oplus G^{u}(y)=T_{y}SM
, by choosing
\epsilon
small enough at the beginning, we see that
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(z_{1})\pitchfork{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(z_{2})
, that is
W^{0s}({\mathcal{O}})\pitchfork W^{0u}({\mathcal{O}})
at some point
z\in U
. It follows that
y\in\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})
. Hence
\mathcal{R}_{1}\subset\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is dense in
SM
by
[
81
, Lemma 7.1]
, the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
C
.

Let
\psi
be Hölder continuous or of the form
\psi^{t}=q\psi^{u}
with
q\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since the geodesic flow
\phi^{t}
is
C^{\infty}
and
\psi
is continuous, the existence of an equilibrium state with respect to
\psi
is
guaranteed by the classical result
[
23
,
85
]
.

For the uniqueness, assume that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are two ergodic equilibrium states with respect to
\psi
.
By assumption,
P(\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c},\psi)<P(\psi)
, we have
\mu_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{1})=\mu_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{1})=1
. Then by Theorem
3.4
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are hyperbolic. Since
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are ergodic, we can take
\lambda>0
small enough so
that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are both
\lambda
-hyperbolic.

Lemma 6.6
.

\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are homoclinically related.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a hyperbolic periodic orbit of
\phi^{t}
and
x\in{\mathcal{O}}
. Let
y_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
be a generic point for
\mu_{i},i=1,2
respectively. As
W^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})={\mathcal{F}}^{0s/0u}({\mathcal{O}})
are dense in
SM
, we have

W^{0s}_{\text{loc}}(y_{1})\pitchfork W^{0u}(x)
and
W^{0u}_{\text{loc}}(y_{2})\pitchfork W^{0s}(x)
. By the inclination lemma, we conclude that
W^{0s}(y_{1})\pitchfork W^{0u}(y_{2})
near
x
. Interchanging the roles of
y_{1},y_{2}
, we also have
W^{0u}(y_{1})\pitchfork W^{0s}(y_{2})
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Applying Theorem
6.3
to
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, we get an irreducible TMF
(\Sigma_{r},\sigma_{r})
and a Hölder continuous map
\pi_{r}:\Sigma_{r}\to SM
such that
\mu_{i}(\pi_{r}[\Sigma_{r}^{\#}])=1,i=1,2
. Therefore,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
lift to ergodic measures
\hat{\mu}_{1}
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}
on
\Sigma_{r}
. These measures are equilibrium states of the potential
\hat{\psi}=\psi\circ\pi_{r}
. Following exactly the same lines as
[
72
, Page 8]
, we can show that
\hat{\psi}
is Hölder continuous.

The measures
\hat{\mu}_{1}
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}
project to ergodic
\sigma
-invariant probability measures
\hat{\nu}_{1}
and
\hat{\nu}_{2}
on the irreducible component
\Sigma
which are equilibrium states of the Hölder continuous potential
\hat{\psi}_{r}-P(\psi)r
where
\hat{\psi}_{r}=\int_{0}^{r({\underline{v}})}\hat{\psi}({\underline{v}},t)dt
. By
[
25
, Theorem 1.1]
, we have
\hat{\nu}_{1}=\hat{\nu}_{2}
, and so
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
.

To prove that the unique equilibrium state
\mu
has full support, note that
\hat{\nu}
has full support in
\Sigma
and then
\hat{\mu}
has full support in
\Sigma_{r}
. Thus
\mu
has full support in
\overline{\pi_{r}(\Sigma_{r})}
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a hyperbolic periodic orbit homoclinically related to
\mu
. Then
\pi_{r}(\Sigma_{r})
is dense in
\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})
. Thus by Lemma
6.5
,

\text{supp}(\mu)=\overline{\pi_{r}(\Sigma_{r})}=\text{HC}({\mathcal{O}})=SM.

By
[
4
, Theorem 1.2]
,
\mu
is Bernoulli, which finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

7.
Counting closed geodesics

Let
M=X/\Gamma
be a closed
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundles, and suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point. We apply the mixing properties of the unique MME of the geodesic flow to prove Theorem
D
. Another essential tool is a type of closing lemma, for which we need to study the dynamics of
\Gamma
on
{\partial X}
.

We do not assume continuous asymptote in Subsections
7.1
and
7.2
.

7.1.
Periodic orbits and deck transformations

Given any isometry
\gamma:X\to X
, the displacement function
d_{\gamma}:X\to X
is defined by
d_{\gamma}(x):=d(x,\gamma x)
. A geodesic
c:{\mathbb{R}}\to X
is called an
axis
of
\gamma\in\Gamma
, if there is a constant
L>0
such that
\gamma c(t)=c(t+L)
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. For
\gamma\in\Gamma
we define
Ax(\gamma)
to be the set of all points which are contained in an axis of
\gamma
.

Lemma 7.1
.

(see
[
36
, Lemma 2.1]
)
Let
M=X/\Gamma
be a compact manifold without conjuagate points and
\gamma\in\Gamma
be a nontrivial element. Then we have:

(1)

d_{\gamma}
assumes a positive minimum,
|\gamma|:=\min d_{\gamma}
.

(2)

The set
Ax(\gamma)
is equal to the set of critical points of
d_{\gamma}
. Furthermore
Ax(\gamma)
is the set where
d_{\gamma}
assumes the minimum.

(3)

For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
|\gamma^{m}|=m|\gamma|
.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
\gamma\in\Gamma
is an axial isometry for both
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
, then
c_{0}(\pm\infty)=c_{1}(\pm\infty)
and the corresponding closed geodesics on
M
lie in the same free homotopy class.

Proof.

Let
C:=\max_{t\in[0,|\gamma|]}d(c_{0}(t),c_{1}(t))
. For any
t_{0}\in[0,|\gamma|]
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle d(c_{0}(t_{0}+n|\gamma|),c_{1}(t_{0}+n|\gamma|))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\gamma^{n}c_{0}(t_{0}),\gamma^{n}c_{1}(t_{0}))=d(c_{0}(t_{0}),c_{1}(t_{0}))\leq C.

It follows that
c_{0}(\pm\infty)=c_{1}(\pm\infty)
, i.e,
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are biasymptotic.

Let us prove that the projections of
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
on
M
lie in the same free homotopy class. Let
s\mapsto c_{s}(0)
be any path from
c_{0}(0)
to
c_{1}(0)
, and define
c_{s}(|\gamma|):=\gamma c_{s}(0)
. This defines
c_{s}(t)
as a continuous function of
(s,t)
on the boundary of
[0,1]\times[0,|\gamma|]
. Since X is simply connected this extends to a continuous map on all of
[0,1]\times[0,|\gamma|]
, and then to
[0,1]\times{\mathbb{R}}
by defining
c_{s}(t\pm|\gamma|):=\gamma^{\pm 1}c_{s}(t)
, this gives the desired homotopy.
∎

According to Lemma
7.2
, for
\gamma\in\Gamma
, denote
\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}:=c(\pm\infty)
where
c
is an axis of
\gamma
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose that
c
is a geodesic on
X
such that
{\underline{c}}=\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}\circ c
is closed. If
\gamma\in\Gamma
fixes
c(-\infty)
and
c(\infty)
, then either
c(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
or
c(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\mp}
.

Proof.

Note that
\gamma c
is a geodesic such that
(\gamma c)(\pm\infty)=c(\pm\infty)
. In fact,
(\gamma^{n}c)(\pm\infty)=c(\pm\infty)
for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus
\gamma^{n}c
lies in a generalized strip (possibly just a single geodesic) connecting
c(\pm\infty)
. Let
c_{\gamma}
be an axis of
\gamma
. Then

\displaystyle d(\gamma^{n}c(0),\gamma^{n}c_{\gamma}(0))=d(c(0),c_{\gamma}(0)).

So
\gamma^{n}c_{\gamma}(0)
lies in a
d(c(0),c_{\gamma}(0))
-neighborhood of the generalized strip. It is clear that
\gamma^{n}c_{\gamma}(0)
converges to
\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
according to
n\to\pm\infty
. We get
c(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\pm}
or
c(\pm\infty)=\xi_{\gamma}^{\mp}
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Given any geodesic
c
on
X
such that
{\underline{c}}=\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}\circ c
is closed, let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
be two axial isometries fixing
c(+\infty)
. Then there exists integers
m,n
such that
\gamma_{1}^{m}=\gamma_{2}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be two axial isometries fixing
c(+\infty)
. Then by Lemma
7.3
and replacing

\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
by their inverses if necessary, we can assume that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
have axes
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
respectively so that
c_{1}(+\infty)=c_{2}(+\infty)=c(+\infty)
. Assume that
\gamma_{1}
translates

c_{1}
by
a>0
and
\gamma_{2}
translates
c_{2}
by
b>0
. For each integer
n\geq 1
let

c_{2}(t_{n})
be the foot of
c_{1}(na)
on
c_{2}
. Then there exists an integer
m=m(n)
such that
|t_{n}-mb|<b
. Now by Proposition
3.7
,

\displaystyle d(c_{1}(0),\gamma_{2}^{-m}\gamma_{1}^{n}c_{1}(0))=d(\gamma_{2}^{m}c_{1}(0),\gamma_{1}^{n}c_{1}(0))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(\gamma_{2}^{m}c_{1}(0),\gamma_{2}^{m}c_{2}(0))+d(c_{2}(mb),c_{2}(t_{n})+d(c_{2}(t_{n}),c_{1}(na))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))+b+Ad(c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))+B.

By proper discontinuity of the action of
\Gamma
on
X
, there exists
n_{1},n_{2}
such that

\gamma_{2}^{-m(n_{1})}\gamma_{1}^{n_{1}}=\gamma_{2}^{-m(n_{2})}\gamma_{1}^{n_{2}}.

So there exists integers
m,n
such that
\gamma_{1}^{m}=\gamma_{2}^{n}
.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

It seems that we can not conclude that the set of
\gamma\in\Gamma
fixing
c(-\infty)
and
c(+\infty)
is an infinite cyclic group as
[
31
, Lemma 2.12]
. The axes of these axial isometries form a generalized strip by Lemmas
7.3
and
3.15
, which is not necessarily a flat strip as in the no focal points case.
[
31
, Lemma 2.12]
makes use of the topological conjugacy between the
\Gamma
-action on
{\partial X}
and the one associated to a metric of negative curvature, which is absent in our setting. That is why we assume continuous asymptote in Theorem
D
in order to prove Lemma
7.20
. See also Remark
7.21
.

7.2.
Neighborhood of an expansive vector

Let
X
be a simply connected
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points, and
\Gamma\subset\text{Iso}(X)
acts proper discontinuously on
X
. We study the dynamics of
\Gamma
on
{\partial X}
, especially in neighborhoods of
v_{0}^{\pm}
in
\overline{X}
where
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. We do not assume that
X
admits a compact quotient
M=X/\Gamma
, and so the results are also applicable to the Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy in the last section.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and
v\in SX
. If there is a constant
c>0
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we can always find a pair of points in the cones:

p_{k}\in C(-v,\frac{1}{k}),~~q_{k}\in C(v,\frac{1}{k}),

with
d(c_{v}(0),c_{p_{k},q_{k}})\geq c
, then
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
79
, Proposition 5]
which essentially uses the cone topology of
\overline{X}
. One can prove that
c_{v}
is the boundary of a generalized strip and thus
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}
.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}\subset SX
. Then for any
\epsilon>0
, there are neighborhoods
U_{\epsilon}
of
c_{v}(-\infty)
and
V_{\epsilon}
of
c_{v}(+\infty)
in
{\partial X}
such that for each pair
(\xi,\eta)\in U_{\epsilon}\times V_{\varepsilon}
, there exists a geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
connecting
\xi
and
\eta
, and for any such geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
we have
d(c_{v}(0),c_{\xi,\eta})<\epsilon
.

Proof.

Since
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
, by Lemma
7.6
, there exist
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and a constant number
c>0
such that for any points
p\in C(-v,\frac{1}{k_{0}})
and
q\in C(v,\frac{1}{k_{0}})
, we have
d(c_{v}(0),c_{p,q})<c
.

Pick up points
p_{i}\in C(-v,\frac{1}{k_{0}})
and
q_{i}\in C(v,\frac{1}{k_{0}})
such that
p_{i}\rightarrow\xi
,
q_{i}\rightarrow\eta
. We choose the parametrization of the geodesic
c_{p_{i},q_{i}}
such that
c_{p_{i},q_{i}}(0)\in B(c_{v}(0),c)
. By the compactness, passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume
w=\lim_{i\rightarrow+\infty}\dot{c}_{p_{i},q_{i}}(0)
. Then
c_{w}(-\infty)=\xi
and
c_{w}(+\infty)=\eta
, and thus
c_{w}
is the geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
we want.

It follows that given any
\epsilon>0
, for sufficiently small neighborhoods
U_{\epsilon}
,
V_{\epsilon}
and all
\xi\in U_{\epsilon}
,
\eta\in V_{\epsilon}
,
there is a geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
connecting
\xi
and
\eta
. Now we prove
d(c_{v}(0),c_{\xi,\eta})<\epsilon
. Suppose this is not true, then we can find a positive number
\epsilon_{0}
and two sequences
\{\xi_{n}\}^{+\infty}_{n=1},\ \{\eta_{n}\}^{+\infty}_{n=1}\subset{\partial X}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,

(1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\xi_{n}=c_{v}(-\infty),~~\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\eta_{n}=c_{v}(+\infty)
,

(2)

The connecting geodesic
c_{\xi_{n},\eta_{n}}
satisfies
d(c_{v}(0),c_{\xi_{n},\eta_{n}})\geq\epsilon_{0}
.

Then by a similar argument of Lemma
7.6
, we can show that
v
is the boundary of a generalized strip, which contradicts to our assumption that
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
.
∎

The following lemma is a consequence of uniform visibility.

Lemma 7.8
.

(
[
31
, Lemma 4.9]
)
Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points. Let
\textbf{Q},\textbf{R}\subset{\partial X}
be disjoint compact sets. Fix a compact subset
A\subset X
and consider for each
T>0
the set

C^{T}_{\textbf{Q}}:=\{c_{p,\xi}(t):p\in A,\xi\in\textbf{Q},t\geq T\},

and define
C^{T}_{\textbf{R}}
similarly.

Then given any open sets
\textbf{U},\textbf{V}\subset{\partial X}
such that
\textbf{U}\supset\textbf{Q}
and
\textbf{V}\supset\textbf{R}
, there exists
T>0
such that if
\gamma\in\Gamma\subset\text{Iso}(X)
satisfies
\gamma(C^{T}_{\textbf{Q}})\cap A\neq\emptyset
and
\gamma(A)\cap C^{T}_{\textbf{R}}\neq\emptyset
, then

\gamma({\partial X}\setminus\textbf{U})\subset\textbf{V }\text{\ and\ }\gamma^{-1}({\partial X}\setminus\textbf{V})\subset\textbf{U}.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
X
be a simply connected uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and let
c
be an axis of
h\in\Gamma\subset\text{Iso}(X)
. For all neighborhood
U\subset\overline{X}
of
c(-\infty)
and
V\subset\overline{X}
of
c(+\infty)
,
there is
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

h^{n}(\overline{X}\setminus U)\subset V,~~h^{-n}(\overline{X}\setminus V)\subset U

for all
