n\geq N
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
7.8
with compact sets
\textbf{Q}\subset U\cap{\partial X}
and
\textbf{R}\subset V\cap{\partial X}
, and
A
a compact neighborhood of
c(0)
. Then there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
h^{n}(C^{T}_{\textbf{Q}})\cap A\neq\emptyset
and
h^{n}(A)\cap C^{T}_{\textbf{R}}\neq\emptyset
for all
n\geq N
. Then

h^{n}({\partial X}\setminus U)\subset V,~~h^{-n}({\partial X}\setminus V)\subset U

for all
n\geq N
.

It remains to consider points in
X\setminus U
. Assume the contrary, then there exists a sequence of points
p_{k}\in X\setminus U
such that
h^{k}p_{k}\to\xi
for some point
\xi\notin V
. This can happen only when
d(c(0),p_{k})\to\infty
since
h
is an axial isometry with endpoint in
V
. Thus we can assume that
p_{k}\to\eta
for some
\eta\in{\partial X}\setminus U
. On the other hand, the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4.9]
uses the requirement
\angle_{q}(\gamma^{-1}x,\eta)>2\epsilon
with
\eta\in{\partial X}
there. We can check that the proof also works for
p_{k}\in X
(instead of
\eta
) satisfying
\angle_{q}(\gamma^{-1}x,p_{k})>2\epsilon
.
\angle_{q}(\gamma^{-1}x,p_{k})>2\epsilon
holds for any
k
large enough. As a consequence of the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4.9]
, we have that there exists
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that
c_{c(0),h^{n}p_{k}}(+\infty)\in V
for any
n\geq N
and any large enough
k
. This contradicts to that
h^{k}p_{k}\to\xi\notin V
. This proves the first inclusion in the proposition.

The second inclusion can be proved analogously, and the proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

7.3.
Flow boxes and a closing lemma

From this subsection, we let
M=X/\Gamma
be a closed
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundles, and suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point. Let
m
denote the unique MME of the geodesic flow, which is also the Bowen-Margulis measure.

We call
v
and
c_{v}
regular if
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Fix a regular vector
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}\subset SX
. Let
p:=\pi(v_{0})
be the reference point in the following discussions. We also fix a scale
\epsilon\in(0,\min\{\frac{1}{8},\frac{{\text{inj}}(M)}{4}\})
.

The
Hopf map

H:SX\to{\partial X}\times{\partial X}\times{\mathbb{R}}
for
p\in X
is defined as

H(v):=(v^{-},v^{+},s(v)),\text{\ where\ }s(v):=b_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p).

From definition, we see

s(\phi^{t}v)=s(v)+t

for any
v\in SX
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
s
is continuous by Corollary
3.12
.

Following
[
31
,
120
]
, we define for each
\theta>0
and
0<\alpha<\frac{3}{2}\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\textbf{P}=\textbf{P}_{\theta}:=\{w^{-}:w\in S_{p}X\text{\ and\ }\angle_{p}(w,v_{0})\leq\theta\},

\displaystyle\textbf{F}=\textbf{F}_{\theta}:=\{w^{+}:w\in S_{p}X\text{\ and\ }\angle_{p}(w,v_{0})\leq\theta\},

\displaystyle B=B_{\theta}^{\alpha}:=H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F}\times[0,\alpha]),

\displaystyle S=S_{\theta}:=B_{\theta}^{\epsilon^{2}}=H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F}\times[0,\epsilon^{2}]).

B=B_{\theta}^{\alpha}
is called a
flow box
with depth
\alpha
. We will consider
\theta>0
small enough, which will be specified in the following.

Recall that
v_{0}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is open. The following is essentially a restatement of Proposition
7.7
in the new setting.

Proposition 7.10
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there is an
\theta_{1}>0
such that, for any
\xi\in\textbf{P}_{\theta_{1}}
and
\eta\in\textbf{F}_{\theta_{1}}
,
there is a unique geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
connecting
\xi
and
\eta
, i.e.,

c_{\xi,\eta}(-\infty)=\xi
and
c_{\xi,\eta}(+\infty)=\eta
.

Moreover, the geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
is regular and
d(c_{v}(0),c_{\xi,\eta})<\epsilon/10
.

Based on Proposition
7.10
, we have the following result.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
v_{0},p,\epsilon
be as above and
\theta_{1}
be given in Proposition
7.10
. Then for any
0<\theta<\theta_{1}
,

(1)

\text{diam\ }\pi H^{-1}(\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\textbf{F}_{\theta}\times\{0\})<\frac{\epsilon}{2}
;

(2)

H^{-1}(\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\textbf{F}_{\theta}\times\{0\})\subset SX
is compact;

(3)

\text{diam\ }\pi B_{\theta}^{\alpha}<4\epsilon
for any
0<\alpha\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{2}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
120
, Lemma 2.13]
, and thus omitted.
∎

At last, we also assume that the choice of
\theta\in(0,\theta_{1})
satisfies the following properties. Since
\theta\mapsto\bar{\mu}(\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\textbf{F}_{\theta})
is nondecreasing and hence has at most countably many discontinuities, we always choose
\theta
to be the continuity point of this function, i.e.,

\lim_{\rho\to\theta}\bar{\mu}(\textbf{P}_{\rho}\times\textbf{F}_{\rho})=\bar{\mu}(\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\textbf{F}_{\theta}).

Furthermore,
\theta
is chosen so that
\bar{\mu}(\partial\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\partial\textbf{F}_{\theta})=0.
By the product structure of
B
and
S
and the definition of
m
, we have for any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{3\epsilon}{2})
,

(20)

\displaystyle\lim_{\rho\to\theta}m(S_{\rho})=m(S_{\theta}),\quad\lim_{\rho\to\theta}m(B^{\alpha}_{\rho})=m(B^{\alpha}_{\theta}),\text{\ \ and\ \ }m(\partial B^{\alpha}_{\theta})=0.

As a consequence of Corollary
3.14
, we have:

Corollary 7.12
.

Given
v_{0},p,\epsilon>0
as above, there exists
\theta_{2}>0
such that for any
0<\theta<\theta_{2}
, if
\xi,\eta\in\textbf{P}_{\theta}
and any
q
lying within
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm diam}}}{\mathcal{F}}+4\epsilon
of
\pi H^{-1}(\textbf{P}_{\theta}\times\textbf{F}_{\theta}\times[0,\infty))
, we have
|b_{\xi}(q,p)-b_{\eta}(q,p)|<\epsilon^{2}
. Similar result holds if the roles of
\textbf{P}_{\theta}
and
\textbf{F}_{\theta}
are reversed.

Let
\theta_{0}:=\min\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}
, where
\theta_{1}
is given in Lemma
7.11
, and
\theta_{2}
is given in Corollary
7.12
. In the following, we always suppose that
0<\theta<\theta_{0}
and (
20
) is satisfied.

Let us collect the definitions of important subsets of
\Gamma
here for convenience.

\displaystyle\Gamma(t,\alpha)=\Gamma_{\theta}(t,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma:S_{\theta}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma_{*}B_{\theta}^{\alpha}\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}=\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma\textbf{F}_{\theta}\subset\textbf{F}_{\theta}\text{\ and\ }\gamma^{-1}\textbf{P}_{\theta}\subset\textbf{P}_{\theta}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\Gamma^{*}\cap\Gamma(t,\alpha),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}(t,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha):\gamma\neq\beta^{n}\text{\ for any\ }\beta\in\Gamma,n\geq 2\}.

Lemma 7.13

(Closing lemma)
.

Let
X
be a simply connected manifold without focal points,
v_{0},p,\epsilon
be fixed as before, and
\theta_{0}
be given as above. Then for every
0<\rho<\theta<\theta_{0}
, there exists some
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
, we have
\Gamma_{\rho}(t,\alpha)\subset\Gamma_{\theta}^{*}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
31
, Lemma 4.8]
, and is based on Lemma
7.8
.
∎

In the following, we use the scaling and mixing properties of Bowen-Margulis measure
m
, to give an asymptotic estimates of
\#\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
and
\#\Gamma(t,\alpha)
.

7.4.
Depth of intersection

Firstly, we collect some results on the relation between
t
and
|\gamma|
when
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
. The proof is analogous to those of
[
31
, Lemmas 4.11, 4.12, 4.13. and 4.14]
, and hence the details are omitted here.

Given
\xi\in{\partial X}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, define
b_{\xi}^{\gamma}:=b_{\xi}(\gamma p,p)
.

Lemma 7.14
.

The following relations hold:

(1)

Let
\xi,\eta\in\textbf{P}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma(t,\alpha)
with
t>0
. Then
|b_{\xi}^{\gamma}-b_{\eta}^{\gamma}|<\epsilon^{2}
.

(2)

Let
c
be an axis of
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\xi=c(-\infty)
. Then
b_{\xi}^{\gamma}=|\gamma|
.

(3)

Given any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

S\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma B^{\alpha}=\{w\in P^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\gamma\textbf{F}):s(w)\in[0,\epsilon^{2}]\cap(b_{w^{-}}^{\gamma}-t+[0,\alpha])\}.

(4)

If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
, then
|\gamma|\in[t-\alpha-\epsilon^{2},t+2\epsilon^{2}]
.

Proof.

The proof of (1) is an application of Corollary
7.12
. The computation is completely parallel to that in
[
31
, Lemma 4.11]
.

The proof of (2) involves computation of Busemann functions, which is completely parallel to that in
[
31
, Lemma 4.12]
.

The proof of (3) is completely parallel to that in
[
31
, Lemma 4.13]
.

The proof of (4) uses (1), (2), (3) and also the fact that
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}
. See the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4.14]
.
∎

The following lemma implies that the intersections also have product structure.

Lemma 7.15
.

If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
, then

S\cap\phi^{-(t+2\epsilon^{2})}\gamma B^{\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}}\supset H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\gamma\textbf{F}\times[0,\epsilon^{2}]):=S^{\gamma}.

Proof.

The proof is completely parallel to that in
[
31
, Lemma 5.1]
. We reproduce the proof since it reflects that advantage of the use of the slice
S
.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
, then
S\cap g^{-t}\gamma B^{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
7.14
(3), there exists
\eta\in\textbf{P}
such that

[0,\epsilon^{2}]\cap(b_{\eta}^{\gamma}-t+[0,\alpha])\neq\emptyset.

It follows that
[0,\epsilon^{2}]\subset(b_{\eta}^{\gamma}-t-\epsilon^{2}+[0,\alpha+2\epsilon^{2}]).
Then by Lemma
7.14
(1), for any
\xi\in\textbf{P}
we have

[0,\epsilon^{2}]\cap(b_{\xi}^{\gamma}-t-\epsilon^{2}+[0,\alpha+2\epsilon^{2}])\neq\emptyset,

which in turn implies that

[0,\epsilon^{2}]\subset(b_{\xi}^{\gamma}-t-2\epsilon^{2}+[0,\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}]).

We are done by Lemma
7.14
(3).
∎

7.5.
Scaling and mixing calculation

The following notations in the asymptotic estimates will be convenient.

\displaystyle f(t)=e^{\pm C}g(t)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow e^{-C}g(t)\leq f(t)\leq e^{C}g(t)\text{\ for all\ }t;

\displaystyle f(t)\lesssim g(t)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\limsup_{t\to\infty}\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}\leq 1;

\displaystyle f(t)\gtrsim g(t)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}\geq 1;

\displaystyle f(t)\sim g(t)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{f(t)}{g(t)}=1;

\displaystyle f(t)\sim e^{\pm C}g(t)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow e^{-C}g(t)\lesssim f(t)\lesssim e^{C}g(t).

Lemma 7.16
.

If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}
, then

m(S^{\gamma})=e^{\pm 2h\epsilon}e^{-h|\gamma|}m(S).

Proof.

The proof is completely parallel to that in
[
31
, Lemma 5.2]
. The main work is to estimate
\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)
and
b_{\eta}(\gamma^{-1}p,p)
given
\xi\in\textbf{P},\eta\in\textbf{F}
.

Firstly, take
q
lying on the geodesic connecting
\xi
and
\eta
such that
b_{\xi}(q,p)=0
. Then

|\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)|=|b_{\xi}(q,p)+b_{\eta}(q,p)|=|b_{\eta}(q,p)|\leq d(q,p)<\frac{\epsilon}{2}

where we used Lemma
7.11
in the last inequality.

Secondly, since
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}
, we know
\gamma^{-1}
has a regular axis
c
, i.e.
\dot{c}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. By Lemma
7.14
(2),
b_{c(-\infty)}(\gamma^{-1}p,p)=|\gamma^{-1}|=|\gamma|
. Then by Corollary
7.12
, we have
|b_{\eta}(\gamma^{-1}p,p)-|\gamma||<\epsilon^{2}
for any
\eta\in\textbf{F}
.

Notice that since
\gamma\textbf{F}\subset\textbf{F}
, we have
\gamma\eta\in\textbf{F}
if
\eta\in\textbf{F}
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\frac{m(S^{\gamma})}{m(S)}=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}\bar{\mu}(\textbf{P}\times\gamma\textbf{F})}{\epsilon^{2}\bar{\mu}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F})}=\frac{e^{\pm h\epsilon/2}\mu_{p}(\textbf{P})\mu_{p}(\gamma\textbf{F})}{e^{\pm h\epsilon/2}\mu_{p}(\textbf{P})\mu_{p}(\textbf{F})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\pm h\epsilon}\frac{\mu_{\gamma^{-1}p}(\textbf{F})}{\mu_{p}(\textbf{F})}=e^{\pm h\epsilon}\frac{\int_{\textbf{F}}e^{-hb_{\eta}(\gamma^{-1}p,p)}d\mu_{p}(\eta)}{\mu_{p}(\textbf{F})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\pm h\epsilon}\epsilon^{\pm h\epsilon^{2}}e^{-h|\gamma|}=e^{\pm 2h\epsilon}e^{-h|\gamma|}.

∎

Combining Lemma
7.14
(4) and Lemma
7.16
, we have

Corollary 7.17

(Scaling)
.

Given
\alpha\leq\frac{3}{2}\epsilon
and
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha)
, we have
|t-|\gamma||\leq 2\epsilon
, and thus

m(S^{\gamma})=e^{\pm 4h\epsilon}e^{-ht}m(S).

Remark 7.18
.

It is clear that the conclusions in Lemma
7.16
and Corollary
7.17
hold if
m,S,S^{\gamma}
are replaced by
{\underline{m}},{\underline{S}},{{\underline{S}}}^{\gamma}
respectively. Recall that we use an underline to denote objects in
M
and
SM
, see Subsection
3.2
.

Finally, we combine scaling and mixing properties of Bowen-Margulis measure to obtain the following asymptotic estimates. The proof is a repetition of
[
31
, Section 5.2]
. Since it is a key step, we provide a detailed proof here.

Proposition 7.19
.

We have

\displaystyle e^{-4h\epsilon}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim e^{4h\epsilon}(1+\frac{4\epsilon^{2}}{\alpha}),

\displaystyle e^{-4h\epsilon}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Gamma_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim e^{4h\epsilon}(1+\frac{4\epsilon^{2}}{\alpha}).

Proof.

Recall that
\alpha\in(0,\frac{3\epsilon}{2}]
. By Lemmas
7.13
and
7.15
, for any
0<\rho<\theta
and
t
large enough, we have

{\underline{S}}_{\rho}\cap\phi^{-t}{\underline{B}}_{\rho}^{\alpha}\subset\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{\underline{S}}_{\theta}^{\gamma}\subset{\underline{S}}_{\theta}\cap\phi^{-(t+2\epsilon^{2})}{\underline{B}}_{\theta}^{\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}}.

By Corollary
7.17
,
{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\theta}^{\gamma})=e^{\pm 4h\epsilon}e^{-ht}{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\theta}).
Thus we have

\displaystyle e^{-4h\epsilon}{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\rho}\cap\phi^{-t}{\underline{B}}_{\rho}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq\#\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)e^{-ht}{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq e^{4h\epsilon}{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\theta}\cap\phi^{-(t+2\epsilon^{2})}{\underline{B}}_{\theta}^{\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}}).

Dividing by
{\underline{m}}({\underline{S}}_{\theta}){\underline{m}}({\underline{B}}_{\theta}^{\alpha})
and using mixing of
{\underline{m}}
, we get

(21)

\displaystyle e^{-4h\epsilon}\frac{m(S_{\rho})m(B_{\rho}^{\alpha})}{m(S_{\theta})m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim\frac{\#\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim e^{4h\epsilon}\frac{m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}})}{m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}.

By (
20
), letting
\rho\nearrow\theta
, we obtain the first line of inequalities in the proposition.

To prove the second line, we consider
\theta<\rho<\theta_{0}
. Then by Lemma
7.13
,
\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)\subset\Gamma_{\theta}(t,\alpha)\subset\Gamma^{*}_{\rho}(t,\alpha).
By (
21
),

\displaystyle e^{-4h\epsilon}\frac{m(S_{\rho})m(B_{\rho}^{\alpha})}{m(S_{\theta})m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\#\Gamma^{*}_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim\frac{\#\Gamma_{\theta}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\#\Gamma^{*}_{\rho}(t,\alpha)}{e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}\lesssim e^{4h\epsilon}\frac{m(B_{\rho}^{\alpha+4\epsilon^{2}})}{m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}.

Letting
\rho\searrow\theta
and by (
20
), we get the second line of inequalities in the proposition.
∎

7.6.
Upper bound for
\#C(t)

Recall that
C(t)
is any maximal set of pairwise non-free-homotopic closed geodesics with length
(t-\epsilon,t]
in
M
(see Theorem
2.10
). We shall obtain an upper bound and a lower bound respectively for
\#C(t)
.

Recall the definition of
\nu_{t}
(see Theorem
2.10
),

\nu_{t}:=\frac{1}{C(t)}\sum_{c\in C(t)}\frac{Leb_{c}}{t}.

Then we have

\#C(t)=\frac{\sum_{{\underline{c}}\in\#C(t)}Leb_{\underline{c}}({\underline{B}}_{\theta}^{\alpha})}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}_{\theta}^{\alpha})}.

Define

\Pi(t):=\{\dot{{\underline{c}}}(s)\in\mathop{\hbox{{\rm pr}}}H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F})\times\{0\}:{\underline{c}}\in C(t),s\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

Then we have

(22)

\displaystyle\#C(t)=\frac{\alpha\#\Pi(t)}{t\nu(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})}.

Now we define a map
\Theta:\Pi(t)\to\Gamma(t,\epsilon)
as follows. Given
{\underline{v}}\in\Pi(t)
, let
\ell=\ell({\underline{v}})\in(t-\epsilon,t]
be such that
\phi^{\ell}{\underline{v}}={\underline{v}}
. Let
v
be the unique lift of
{\underline{v}}
such that
v\in H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F})\times\{0\}\subset B_{\theta}^{\alpha}
.
Define
\Theta(v)
to be the unique axial isometry of
X
such that
\phi^{\ell}v=\Theta(v)v
. Then
|\Theta(v)|=\ell
. If
\gamma=\Theta(v)
, then

\phi^{t}v=\phi^{t-\ell}\gamma v\in\gamma B_{\theta}^{\epsilon},

i.e.,
v\in S_{\theta}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma B_{\theta}^{\epsilon}
. So we get

(23)

\displaystyle\Theta(\Pi(t))\subset\Gamma(t,\epsilon).

To estimate
\#\Pi(t)
, we first show that
\Theta
is injective.

Lemma 7.20
.

\Theta
is injective.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\underline{v}},{\underline{w}}\in\Pi(t)
are such that
\Theta({\underline{v}})=\Theta({\underline{w}}):=\gamma
.
Let
v,w\in B_{\theta}^{\alpha}
be the lifts of
{\underline{v}},{\underline{w}}
respectively. Then by definition, both
c_{v}
and
c_{w}
are axes of
\gamma
. By Lemma
7.2
,
v^{+}=w^{+}
and
v^{-}=w^{-}
.

It follows that
c_{v}
and
c_{w}
are bi-asymptotic. If
c_{v}
and
c_{w}
are geometrically distinct, then they bound a generalized strip by Lemma
3.15
. So
v
and
w
are singular vectors, which is a contradiction by the definition of
B_{\theta}^{\alpha}
and Proposition
7.10
. So
v
and
w
lie on a common geodesic. As
v,w\in H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F}\times\{0\})
, we have
v=w
and hence
{\underline{v}}={\underline{w}}
. So
\Theta
is injective.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

Lemma
7.20
essentially uses that our flow box is contained in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
by Proposition
7.10
. If we take the center of the flow box inside
\mathcal{R}_{0}
, then the flow box might contain non-expansive vectors. In this case, if we follow the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4.3]
, we need use
[
31
, Lemma 2.12]
, which is not available in our setting. See Remark
7.5
.

Proposition 7.22
.

We have

\#C(t)\leq\frac{\epsilon\#\Gamma(t,\epsilon)}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\epsilon})}.

Proof.

The proposition follows from (
22
), (
23
) and Lemma
7.20
.
∎

7.7.
Lower bound for
\#C(t)

First we deal with the multiplicity of
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Given
\gamma\in\Gamma
, let
d=d(\gamma)\in{\mathbb{N}}
be maximal such that
\gamma=\beta^{d}
for some
\beta\in\Gamma
.
\gamma\in\Gamma
is called
primitive
if
d(\gamma)=1
, i.e.,
\gamma\neq\beta^{d}
for any
\beta\in\Gamma
and any
d\geq 2
.

Define
\Gamma_{2}(\textbf{P},\textbf{F},t)
to be the set of all
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that

(1)

\gamma
has an axis
c
with
c(-\infty)\in\textbf{P},c(\infty)\in\textbf{F}
;

(2)

|\gamma|\in(t-\epsilon,t]
;

(3)

d(\gamma)\geq 2
.

Lemma 7.23
.

There exists
K>0
such that for any
t>0
we have

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{2}(\textbf{P},\textbf{F},t)}d(\gamma)\leq Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht}.

Proof.

By Freire and Mañé’s theorem (cf.
[
45
]
), the topological entropy of the geodesic flow coincides with the volume entropy, i.e., for any
x\in X

h=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\text{Vol}B(x,r)}{r}

where
B(x,r)
is the open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
in
X
. Based on this fact, the proof is almost analogous to that in
[
31
, Lemma 4.5]
with only minor modifications as follows.

If
\gamma=\beta(\gamma)^{d(\gamma)}
for some
\beta(\gamma)\in\Gamma
, we must argue that
\beta(\gamma)
has an axis with endpoints in
P
and
F
, so that we can choose
v\in H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F}\times\{0\})
tangent to such an axis with
\phi^{|\beta(\gamma)|}v=\beta(\gamma)v
. We cannot use
[
31
, Lemma 2.10]
in our setting. Nevertheless, since
\gamma
has a regular axis with endpoints in
P
and
F
, we know from the proof of Lemma
7.20
that this is the only axis for
\gamma
. Every axis of
\beta(\gamma)
is an axis of
\gamma
, so
\beta(\gamma)
also has a unique axis, which is the axis of
\gamma
, as we want.

Repeat the remaining part of the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4.5]
and we are done.
∎

Recall that
\Gamma^{\prime}(t,\alpha):=\{\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha):\gamma\neq\beta^{n}\text{\ for any\ }\beta\in\Gamma,n\geq 2\}
.

Lemma 7.24
.

Consider
\alpha=\epsilon-4\epsilon^{2}
. Then
\Theta(\Pi(t))\supset\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)
and

\#C(t)\geq\frac{\alpha\#\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)
.
Then there exists
v\in H^{-1}(\textbf{P}\times\textbf{F}\times\{0\})
such that
\phi^{|\gamma|}v=\gamma v
.
By Lemma
7.14
(4), we have

\displaystyle|\gamma|

\displaystyle\geq(t-2\epsilon^{2})-\alpha-\epsilon^{2}=t-\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}>t-\epsilon,

\displaystyle|\gamma|

\displaystyle\leq(t-2\epsilon^{2})+2\epsilon^{2}=t.

Since
\gamma
is primitive,
\underline{c}_{{\underline{v}}}
is a closed geodesic with length
|\gamma|\in(t-\epsilon,t]
.
Note that if
\underline{c}
is another closed geodesic in the free-homotopic class of
\underline{c}_{{\underline{v}}}
, then we can lift
\underline{c}
to a geodesic
c
such that
c
and
c_{v}
are bi-asymptotic. So
c
and
c_{v}
bound a generalized strip by Lemma
3.15
, which is a contradiction since
v
is regular. It follows that
\underline{c}_{{\underline{v}}}
is the only geodesic in its free-homotopic class. Thus
\underline{c}_{{\underline{v}}}\in C(t)
.

As a consequence,
v\in\Pi(t)
and
\gamma=\Theta(v)
. So
\Theta(\Pi(t))\supset\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)
and thus by (
22
),

\#C(t)\geq\frac{\alpha\#\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}.

∎

Proposition 7.25
.

Consider
\alpha=\epsilon-4\epsilon^{2}
. We have

\#C(t)\geq\frac{\alpha}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}\cdot(\#\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)-Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht}).

Proof.

From the proof of Lemma
7.24
, we also see that
|\gamma|\in(t-\epsilon,t]
if
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)
. Thus

\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)\setminus\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)\subset\Gamma_{2}(\textbf{P},\textbf{F},t).

Then by Lemma
7.23
,

\#\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)-\#\Gamma^{\prime}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)\leq Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht}.

This together with Lemma
7.24
proves the proposition.
∎

7.8.
Equidistribution and completion of the proof

The following result is standard in ergodic theory, which is a corollary of the classical proof of variational principle
[
115
, Theorem 9.10]
. The flow version is given in
[
43
, Proposition 4.3.14]
.

Lemma 7.26
.

Let
Y
be a compact metric space and
\{\phi^{t}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
a continuous flow on
Y
. Fix
\epsilon>0
and suppose that
E_{t}\subset Y
is a
(t,\epsilon)
-separated set for all sufficiently large
t
. Define the measures
\mu_{t}
by

\mu_{t}(A):=\frac{1}{\#E_{t}}\sum_{v\in E_{t}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{A}(\phi^{s}v)ds.

If
t_{k}\to\infty
and the weak
∗
limit
\mu=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{t_{k}}

exists, then

h_{\mu}(\phi^{1})\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{t_{k}}\log\#E_{t_{k}}.

Proof of Theorem
2.10
.

First we prove the following claim.

Claim:
The set
\{\underline{\dot{c}}(0):c\in C(t)\}
is
(t,\epsilon)
-separated for any
0<\epsilon<{\text{inj}}(M)/2
.

Assume the contrary, namely,
C(t)
contains two closed geodesics
\underline{c}_{1},\underline{c}_{2}
in distinct free-homotopic classes
such that
d(\underline{c}_{1}(s),\underline{c}_{2}(s))\leq\epsilon<{\text{inj}}(M)/2
for all
s\in[0,t]
. Define
{\underline{v}}=\underline{\dot{c}}_{1}(0)
and
{\underline{w}}=\underline{\dot{c}}_{2}(0)
.
We can lift
{\underline{v}},{\underline{w}}
to
v,w\in SX
, and
\underline{c}_{1},\underline{c}_{2}
to
c_{1},c_{2}
respectively, such that
d(c_{1}(s),c_{2}(s))\leq\epsilon
for all
s\in[0,t]
.
Moreover, there exist
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in(t-\epsilon,t]
such that
\gamma_{1}v=\phi^{t_{1}}v
and
\gamma_{2}w=\phi^{t_{2}}w
. Then

\displaystyle d(\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{1}c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))=

\displaystyle d(\gamma_{2}^{-1}c_{1}(t_{1}),\gamma_{2}^{-1}c_{2}(t_{2}))=d(c_{1}(t_{1}),c_{2}(t_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d(c_{1}(t_{1}),c_{2}(t_{1}))+|t_{2}-t_{1}|\leq\epsilon+2\epsilon=3\epsilon.

Hence
d(\gamma_{2}^{-1}\gamma_{1}c_{1}(0),c_{2}(0))\leq 3\epsilon<2{\text{inj}}(M)
, which is possible only if
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
. Then
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are both axes for a common
\gamma:=\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
. By Lemma
7.2
, we see that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
must be bi-asymptotic and that
\underline{c}_{1}
and
\underline{c}_{2}
are free-homotopic. A contradiction, so the claim holds.

Now by Propositions
7.25
and
7.19
, we know

\displaystyle\#C(t)\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}\cdot(\#\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)-Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht})

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{t\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}\cdot(e^{-4h\epsilon}e^{ht}m(B_{\theta}^{\alpha})-Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht}).

So
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\#C(t)\geq h.
Applying Lemma
7.26
, we know any limit measure of
\nu_{t}
has entropy equal to
h
, and thus it must be
m
, the unique MME. This proves Theorem
2.10
.
∎

Proposition 7.27
.

We have

\#C(t)\sim e^{\pm Q\epsilon}\frac{\epsilon}{t}e^{ht}

where
Q>0
is a universal constant depending only on
h
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.10
and (
20
), we have
\nu_{t}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})\to{\underline{m}}({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})=m(B^{\alpha})
for
\alpha=\epsilon
and
\alpha=\epsilon-4\epsilon^{2}
.

(1)

Consider
\alpha=\epsilon
. By Propositions
7.22
and
7.19
, we have

\#C(t)\lesssim\frac{\epsilon\#\Gamma(t,\epsilon)}{tm({\underline{B}}^{\epsilon})}\lesssim e^{4h\epsilon}(1+4\epsilon)\frac{\epsilon}{t}e^{ht}.

(2)

Now consider
\alpha=\epsilon-4\epsilon^{2}
. By Propositions
7.25
and
7.19
,

\displaystyle\#C(t)\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{tm({\underline{B}}^{\alpha})}\cdot(\#\Gamma^{*}(t-2\epsilon^{2},\alpha)-Ke^{\frac{2}{3}ht})

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle(1-4\epsilon)e^{-4h\epsilon}\frac{\epsilon}{t}e^{-2h\epsilon^{2}}e^{ht}.

As
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{8}
, there exists a universal
Q>0
depending only on
h
such that

\#C(t)\sim e^{\pm Q\epsilon}\frac{\epsilon}{t}e^{ht}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
D
.

The last step of the proof of Theorem
D
is to estimate
\#P(t)
via
\#C(t)
using a Riemannian sum argument.
Indeed, a verbatim repetition of the proof in
[
31
, Section 6.2]
gives

\#P(t)\sim e^{\pm 2(Q+h)\epsilon}\frac{e^{ht}}{ht}.

Since
\epsilon>0
can be arbitrarily small, we get
\#P(t)\sim\frac{e^{ht}}{ht}
which completes the proof of Theorem
D
.
∎

8.
Volume Asymptotics

Let
M=X/\Gamma
be a closed
C^{\infty}
visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundles. Suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point. We prove Theorem
E
in this section, that is,

b_{t}(x)/\frac{e^{ht}}{h}\sim c(x),

where
b_{t}(x)
is the Riemannian volume of the ball of radius
t>0
around
x\in X
,
h
the topological entropy of the geodesic flow, and
c:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a continuous function. The strategy is to use the mixing properties of the MME to count the number of intersections of a flow box under the geodesic flow with another flow box. However, we have to deal with countably many pairs of flow boxes centered at regular vectors.

8.1.
Regular partition-cover and local uniform expansion

We first prove two auxiliary lemmas which demonstrate that regular vectors are abundant inside stable horospherical manifolds and unit tangent spheres.

Lemma 8.1
.

Assume that
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
is a recurrent vector, then any
w\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
is in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

Proof.

First of all, since
v
is a recurrent vector in
SX
, then we can find a sequence
\{\alpha_{n}\}^{\infty}_{n=1}\subset\Gamma
and a sequence of time
\{t_{n}\}^{\infty}_{n=1}
with
t_{n}\rightarrow+\infty
, such that

(24)

d\alpha_{n}(\phi^{t_{n}}v)\to v,~~~n\to+\infty.

Now take an arbitrary vector
w\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
. Assume the contrary that
w\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
. By Proposition
3.7
, the function
t\to d(\phi^{t}(w),\phi^{t}(v))
is bounded above by some constant
C>0
.
Then for each
t_{n}
in (
24
), we have for any
s\in[-t_{n},+\infty)
,

(25)

\displaystyle d(\phi^{t_{n}+s}(w),\phi^{t_{n}+s}(v))=d(d\alpha_{n}\circ\phi^{t_{n}+s}w,d\alpha_{n}\circ\phi^{t_{n}+s}v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d(\phi^{s}\circ d\alpha_{n}\circ\phi^{t_{n}}w,\phi^{s}\circ d\alpha_{n}\circ\phi^{t_{n}}v).

Consider
s=0
in (
25
). Note that
d(\phi^{t_{n}}(w),\phi^{t_{n}}(v))\leq C
, and
d\alpha_{n}(\phi^{t_{n}}v)\rightarrow v
.
So we know that for any
\epsilon>0
there is an integer
N>0
such that for any
n>N
,

d(v,d\alpha_{n}(\phi^{t_{n}}w))\leq C+\epsilon.

This implies that the set
\{d\alpha_{n}(\phi^{t_{n}}w)\}
has an accumulate point. Without loss of generality, we assume
d\alpha_{n}(\phi^{t_{n}}w)\rightarrow w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
as
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
is a closed subset.

Letting
n\rightarrow+\infty
in (
25
), we get that

d(\phi^{s}(w^{\prime}),\phi^{s}(v))\leq C,~~~\forall~s\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
w^{\prime}\neq v
, it follows that the geodesics
c_{v}
and
c_{w^{\prime}}
bound a nontrivial generalized strip. Then
v\in\mathcal{R}_{0}^{c}\subset\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
which contradicts to the assumption that
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. We are done with the proof.
∎

Recall that for any
x\in X
the bijection
f_{x}:S_{x}X\to{\partial X}
is defined by
f_{x}(v)=v^{+},v\in S_{x}X
. Let
\tilde{\mu}_{x}:=(f_{x}^{-1})_{*}\mu_{p}
which is a finite Borel measure on
S_{x}X
.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
x\in X
, we have
\tilde{\mu}_{x}(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cap S_{x}X)=\tilde{\mu}_{x}(S_{x}X)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Rec}}}\subset SM
be the subset of vectors recurrent under the geodesic flow. Then its lift to
SX
, which is also denoted by
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Rec}}}
, has full
m
-measure. By assumption of Theorem
E
and Corollary C.1,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
also has full
m
-measure, and thus
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Rec}}}\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}
has full
m
-measure. Define
R:=\{v^{+}:v\in\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Rec}}}\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}\}
. By definition of the Bowen-Margulis measure, we see that
R
has full
\mu_{x}
-measure.

Let
v\in\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Rec}}}\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}
. By Lemma
8.1
, for every
w\in{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
, we have
w\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. It follows that
R\cap\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Sing}}}^{+}=\emptyset
where
\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Sing}}}^{+}:=\{v^{+}:v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}\}
. So
\mu_{x}(\mathop{\hbox{{\rm Sing}}}^{+})=0
. The lemma then follows.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a fundamental domain of
\Gamma
. Fix
x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}\subset X
, and
p=x
the reference point. For each regular vector
w\in S_{x}X\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}
, we can construct a local product flow box around
w
as in Subsection 7.3. More precisely, consider the interior of
B_{\theta}^{\alpha}(w)
,
\text{int}B_{\theta}^{\alpha}(w)
, which is an open neighborhood of
w
for some
\alpha>0
and
0<\theta<\theta_{0}
(here
\theta_{0}
depends on
w
). By second countability of
S_{x}X
, there exist countably many regular vectors
w_{1},w_{2},\cdots
such that
S_{y}X\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}\text{int}B_{\theta_{i}}^{\alpha}(w_{i})
.
Similarly, there exist countably many regular vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\cdots
such that
S_{y}X\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}\text{int}B_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\alpha}(v_{i})
. We note that the reference point
p
is always chosen to be
x
in the construction of all above flow boxes.

A
regular partition-cover
of
S_{x}X
is a triple
(\{w_{i}\},\{\text{int}B_{\theta_{i}}^{\alpha}(w_{i})\},\{N_{i}\})
where
\{N_{i}\}
is a disjoint partition of
\mathcal{R}_{1}\cap S_{x}X
and such that
N_{i}\subset\text{int}B_{\theta_{i}}^{\alpha}(w_{i})
for each
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Similarly a regular partition-cover of
S_{y}X
is a triple
(\{v_{i}\},\{\text{int}B_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\alpha}(v_{i})\},\{V_{i}\})
such that
\{V_{i}\}
is a disjoint partition of
\mathcal{R}_{1}\cap S_{y}X
and
V_{i}\subset\text{int}B_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\alpha}(v_{i})
for each
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

In the next subsection, we will first consider a pair of
V_{i}
and
N_{j}
from the regular partition-covers of
S_{x}X
and
S_{y}X
respectively. At last, we will sum up our estimates over countably many such pairs from regular partition-covers.

Consider a pair of
V_{i}
and
N_{j}
from the regular partition-covers of
S_{x}X
and
S_{y}X
respectively. For simplicity, we just denote
V:=V_{i}
and
N:=N_{j}
. Then
N\subset\text{int}B_{\theta_{j}}^{\alpha}(w_{0})
and
V\subset\text{int}B_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\beta}(v_{0})
for some
w_{0}\in S_{x}X
and
v_{0}\in S_{y}X
. We also denote for
a>0
,

\displaystyle B_{a}N

\displaystyle:=\{v\in S_{x}X:d(v,N)\leq a\}

\displaystyle B_{-a}N

\displaystyle:=\{v\in N:B(v,a)\subset N\}.

Write
t_{0}:=s(v_{0})=b_{v_{0}^{-}}(\pi v_{0},p)
where
p=x
. We denote the flow boxes by

\displaystyle N^{\alpha}:=H^{-1}(N^{-}\times N^{+}\times[-\alpha,\alpha]),

\displaystyle V^{\beta}:=H^{-1}(V^{-}\times V^{+}\times(t_{0}+[-\beta,\beta])).

Notice that
N^{\alpha}\subset\text{int}B_{\theta_{j}}^{\alpha}(w_{0})
and
V^{\beta}\subset\text{int}B_{\theta^{\prime}_{i}}^{\beta}(v_{0})
.
Given
\epsilon>0
, we always consider
\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{100}\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{2}
.
By carefully adjusting the regular partition-covers, we can guarantee that

(26)

\displaystyle\mu_{p}(\partial V^{+})=\mu_{p}(\partial V^{-})=\mu_{p}(\partial N^{+})=\mu_{p}(\partial N^{-})=0.

In the next subsection, we will count the number of elements in certain subsets of
\Gamma
. Let us collect the definitions here for convenience.

\displaystyle\Gamma(t,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma:N^{\alpha}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V^{\beta}\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{-\rho}(t,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma:(B_{-\rho}N)^{\alpha}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma(B_{-\rho}V)^{\beta}\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma V^{+}\subset N^{+}\text{\ and\ }\gamma^{-1}N^{-}\subset V^{-}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle:=\Gamma^{*}\cap\Gamma(t,\alpha,\beta).

The notation here is similar to that in Subsection 7.3. However, it should not cause any confusion.

Lemma 8.3
.

For every
\rho>0
, there exists some
T_{2}>0
such that for all
t\geq T_{2}
, we have
\Gamma_{-\rho}(t,\alpha,\beta)\subset\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha,\beta)
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
7.8
with
\textbf{Q}=(B_{-\rho}V)^{-}
,
\textbf{R}=(B_{-\rho}N)^{+}
,
A=\pi\overline{(B_{-\rho}N)^{\alpha}\cup(B_{-\rho}V)^{\beta}}
, and
\textbf{V},\textbf{U}
be the interiors of
N^{+}
and
V^{-}
respectively. Then
(B_{-\rho}N)^{+}\subset\textbf{V}
and
(B_{-\rho}V)^{-}\subset\textbf{U}
. Let
T
be given by Lemma
7.8
. Then for every
t\geq T
and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{-\rho}(t,\alpha,\beta)
, there exists
v\in(B_{-\rho}N)^{\alpha}
such that
w=\gamma_{*}^{-1}\phi^{t}v\in(B_{-\rho}V)^{\beta}
. Putting
z_{1}=\pi v\in A
, then

\gamma^{-1}z_{1}=\pi\gamma_{*}^{-1}v=\pi\phi^{-t}w.

Since
\pi w\in A
,
w^{-}\in(B_{-\rho}V)^{-}=\textbf{Q}
,
t\geq T
, we see that
\gamma^{-1}z_{1}\in C_{\textbf{Q}}^{T}
.

Similarly, putting
z_{2}=\pi w\in A
, we have

\gamma z_{2}=\pi\gamma_{*}w=\pi\phi^{t}v.

Since
\pi v\in A
,
v^{+}\in(B_{-\rho}N)^{+}=\textbf{R}
and
t\geq T
, we see that
\gamma z_{2}\in C_{\textbf{R}}^{T}
. The lemma then follows from Lemma
7.8
.
∎

8.2.
Using scaling and mixing

In this subsection, we use the scaling and mixing properties of Bowen-Margulis measure
m
, to give an asymptotic estimates of
\#\Gamma^{*}(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)
and
\#\Gamma(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)
.

8.2.1.
Intersection components

We need some delicate estimates on numbers of intersection components. Note that
N
is on the unit tangent sphere
S_{x}X
, which is different from
H^{-1}(N^{-}\times N^{+})\times\{0\}.

The following is obtained in
[
119
, Lemmas 5.1 and 5.2]
.

Lemma 8.4
.

We have:

(1)

N\subset N^{\epsilon^{2}}
,
V\subset V^{\epsilon^{2}}
.

(2)

For any
t>0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\{\gamma\in\Gamma:N^{\epsilon}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V\neq\emptyset\}\subset\{\gamma\in\Gamma:N^{\epsilon^{2}}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V^{\epsilon}\neq\emptyset\}.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
p\in X
, then given any
a>0,\epsilon>0
, there exists
T>0
such that for any
t\geq T
and
v,w\in S_{p}X
,
d(\phi^{t}v,\phi^{t}w)\leq\epsilon
implies that
\angle(v,w)<a
.

Proof.

The lemma is a direct consequence of uniform visibility property. Indeed, let
L(a)
be the constant from uniform visibility property. Let
T
be large enough so that
T-\epsilon>L(a)
and
t\geq T
. Then by the triangle inequality, the geodesic connecting
\phi^{t}v
and
\phi^{t}w
stays at distance at least

2t-\epsilon\geq 2T-\epsilon>L(a)

from
p
. Then
\angle(v,w)<a
by uniform visibility.
∎

Based on Lemmas
8.4
(1),
7.11
,
8.5
, by a proof parallel to
[
119
, Lemma 5.4]
, we have

Lemma 8.6
.

For any
a>0
, there exists
T_{1}>0
large enough such that for any
t\geq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\{\gamma\in\Gamma:N^{\alpha}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V^{\beta}\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\{\gamma\in\Gamma:(B_{a}N)^{\alpha+\beta+\epsilon^{2}}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V\neq\emptyset\}.

8.2.2.
Depth of intersection

Given
\xi\in{\partial X}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, define
b_{\xi}^{\gamma}:=b_{\xi}(\gamma p,p)
.
Using Corollary
7.12
, we have

Lemma 8.7
.

(
[
119
, Lemma 5.5]
)
Let
\xi,\eta\in N^{-}
, and
\gamma\in\Gamma(t,\alpha,\beta)
with
t>0
. Then
|b_{\xi}^{\gamma}-b_{\eta}^{\gamma}|<\epsilon^{2}
.

The following two lemmas give estimate on the depth of intersections, and implies that the intersection components also have product structure.

Lemma 8.8
.

(
[
119
, Lemma 5.6]
)
Given any
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\alpha,\beta)
and any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle N^{\epsilon^{2}}\cap\phi^{-t}\gamma V^{\beta}=\{

\displaystyle w\in E^{-1}(N^{-}\times\gamma V^{+}):

\displaystyle s(w)\in[-\epsilon^{2},\epsilon^{2}]\cap(b_{w^{-}}^{\gamma}-t+t_{0}+[-\beta,\beta])\}

where
t_{0}=s(v_{0})
.

Lemma 8.9
.

(
[
119
, Lemma 5.7]
)
If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)
, then

N^{\epsilon^{2}}\cap\phi^{-(t+4\epsilon^{2})}\gamma V^{\beta+8\epsilon^{2}}\supset H^{-1}(N^{-}\times\gamma V^{+}\times[-\epsilon^{2},\epsilon^{2}]):=N^{\gamma}.

8.2.3.
Scaling and mixing calculation

The following result is obtained using the product structure of Bowen-Margulis measure
m
. It says that the measure of an intersection component decreases with a factor
e^{-ht}
due to the contraction of
\phi^{-t}
in the unstable direction.

Lemma 8.10
.

(
[
119
, Lemma 5.8]
)
If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{*}(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)
, then

\frac{m(N^{\gamma})}{m(V^{\beta})}=e^{\pm 26h\epsilon}e^{ht_{0}}e^{-ht}\frac{\epsilon^{2}\mu_{p}(N^{-})}{\beta\mu_{p}(V^{-})}

where
N^{\gamma}
is from Lemma
8.9
.

Finally, we can combine scaling and mixing properties of Bowen-Margulis measure to obtain the following asymptotic estimates.

Proposition 8.11
.

(
[
119
, Proposition 5.9]
)
We have

\displaystyle e^{-30h\epsilon}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Gamma^{*}(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)e^{ht_{0}}}{\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})e^{ht}}\frac{1}{2\beta}\lesssim e^{30h\epsilon}(1+\frac{8\epsilon^{2}}{\beta}),

\displaystyle e^{-30h\epsilon}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\#\Gamma(t,\epsilon^{2},\beta)e^{ht_{0}}}{\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})e^{ht}}\frac{1}{2\beta}\lesssim e^{30h\epsilon}(1+\frac{8\epsilon^{2}}{\beta}).

8.2.4.
Integration

Let
V\subset S_{y}X,N\subset S_{x}X
be as above, and
0\leq a<b
. Let
n(a,b,V,N^{0})
denote the number of connected components at which
\phi^{[-b,-a]}{\underline{V}}
intersects
{\underline{N}}^{0}
, and
n_{t}(V^{\beta},N^{\alpha})
(resp.
n_{t}(V,N^{\alpha})
) denote the number of connected components at which
\phi^{-t}{\underline{V}}^{\beta}
(resp.
\phi^{-t}{\underline{V}}
) intersects
{\underline{N}}^{\alpha}
.

Applying Lemma
8.4
(2) and Proposition
8.11
, we have

Lemma 8.12
.

n_{t}(V,N^{\epsilon})\lesssim e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})e^{ht}2\epsilon(1+8\epsilon)e^{30h\epsilon}.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
8.4
(2) with
\alpha=\epsilon^{2}
, we have

n_{t}(V,N^{\epsilon})\leq n_{t}(V^{\epsilon},N^{\epsilon^{2}}).

Letting
\beta=\epsilon
in Proposition
8.11
, we have

\displaystyle n_{t}(V^{\epsilon},N^{\epsilon^{2}})=\#\Gamma(t,\epsilon^{2},\epsilon)\lesssim

\displaystyle e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})e^{ht}2\epsilon(1+8\epsilon)e^{30h\epsilon}.

∎

Lemma 8.13
.

One has

n_{t}(V,N^{\epsilon})\gtrsim e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})e^{ht}2\epsilon(1-2\epsilon)e^{-30h\epsilon}.

Proof.

Setting
\alpha=\epsilon^{2}
,
\beta=\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2}
in Lemma
8.6
, for any
a>0
, we have

n_{t}(V^{\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2}},(B_{-a}N)^{\epsilon^{2}})\leq n_{t}(V,N^{\epsilon})

for any
t\geq T_{1}
where
T_{1}
is provided by Lemma
8.6
.
Applying Proposition
8.11
with
\beta=\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle n_{t}(V^{\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2}},(B_{-a}N)^{\epsilon^{2}})\gtrsim

\displaystyle e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}((B_{-a}N)^{+})e^{ht}2\epsilon(1-2\epsilon)e^{-30h\epsilon}.

Letting
a\to 0
, by (
26
) we obtain the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Combining Lemmas
8.12
and
8.13
, using standard integration technique, we have

Proposition 8.14
.

There exists
Q>0
such that

\displaystyle e^{-2Q\epsilon}e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}

\displaystyle\lesssim n(0,t,V,N^{0})

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{2Q\epsilon}e^{-ht_{0}}\mu_{p}(V^{-})\mu_{p}(N^{+})\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}.

8.3.
Summing over the regular partition-cover

Denote

a_{t}(x,y):=\#\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma y\in B(x,t)\}

and

a^{1}_{t}(x,y):=\#\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma y\in B(x,t),\text{\ and\ }\dot{c}_{x,\gamma y}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}\}.

It is easy to see that
b_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{F}}a_{t}(x,y)d\text{Vol}(y)
.

The following shows that
a_{t}^{1}(x,y)
is not dominating in the asymptotic estimates. The proof essentially uses the entropy gap.

Lemma 8.15
.

There exist
C>0
and
0<h^{\prime}<h
, such that for any
x,y\in{\mathcal{F}}
,

a^{1}_{t}(x,y)\leq Ce^{h^{\prime}t}.

Proof.

Given any
\epsilon<{\text{inj}}M/5
and
t>0
, let
\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma
be such that
\gamma_{1}y,\gamma_{2}y\in B(x,t)\setminus B(x,t-\epsilon)
, and
\dot{c}_{x,\gamma_{1}y}(0),\dot{c}_{x,\gamma_{2}y}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c}
. Then it is easy to see that
\dot{c}_{x,\gamma_{1}y}(0)
and
\dot{c}_{x,\gamma_{2}y}(0)
are
(t,\epsilon)
-separated. By the uniqueness of MME and the fact
m(\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c})=0
(see Corollary C.1), we have the entropy gap:
h_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm top}}}(\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c})<h
. It follows that the number of
\gamma\in\Gamma
as above is less than
C_{1}e^{h^{\prime}t}
for some
C_{1}>0
and
h_{\mathop{\hbox{\footnotesize\rm top}}}(\mathcal{R}_{1}^{c})<h^{\prime}<h
.

Let
t_{i}=i\epsilon
, then

a^{1}_{t}(x,y)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{[t/\epsilon]+1}C^{\prime}e^{h^{\prime}t_{i}}=\frac{C^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{[t/\epsilon]+1}\epsilon e^{h^{\prime}t_{i}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t+\epsilon}e^{h^{\prime}s}ds\leq Ce^{h^{\prime}t}

for some
C>0
.
∎

Denote by

a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j}):=\#\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma y\in\pi\phi^{[0,t]}\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j}\},

and similarly,

a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{i=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i}):=\#\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma^{-1}x\in\pi\phi^{[-t,0]}\cup_{i=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i}\}.

Applying the shadow lemma, Proposition
5.8
(3), we have the following inequality:

Lemma 8.16
.

(
[
119
, Lemma 5.14]
)
There exists
C>0
such that

\limsup_{t\to\infty}e^{-ht}a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j})\leq C\cdot\mu_{p}((\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j})^{+}),

and

\limsup_{t\to\infty}e^{-ht}a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{i=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i})\leq C\cdot\mu_{p}((\cup_{i=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i})^{-}).

Proof of Theorem
E
.

Since the diameter of each flow box is no more than
4\epsilon
, we have

(27)

\displaystyle n(0,t,\cup V_{i},\cup(N_{j})^{0})\leq a_{t+4\epsilon}(x,y)

and

(28)

\displaystyle a_{t-4\epsilon}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq a^{1}_{t-4\epsilon}(x,y)+a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j})

\displaystyle+a(0,t,x,y,\cup_{i=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i})+n(0,t,\cup_{i=1}^{m}V_{i},\cup_{j=1}^{n}(N_{j})^{0}).

For each
V_{i}
, denote by
t_{0}^{i}:=b_{v_{i}^{-}}(\pi v_{i},p)
where
v_{i}\in V_{i}
. Recall that in Subsection 8.1 we suppressed
i
and write
t_{0}=t_{0}^{i}
, since only one
V_{i}
is considered there. By Proposition
8.14
, for any
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\infty}e^{-ht}n(0,t,\cup V_{i},\cup(N_{j})^{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\infty}e^{-ht}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}n(0,t,V_{i},(N_{j})^{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e^{-2Q\epsilon}e^{-hb_{v_{i}^{-}}(\pi v_{i},p)}\mu_{p}((V_{i})^{-})\mu_{p}((N_{j})^{+})\frac{1}{h}.

Note that
v\mapsto b_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)
is a continuous function by Corollary
3.12
. So if we choose a sequence of finer and finer regular partition-covers, and let
m,n\to\infty
on the right hand,

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\infty}e^{-ht}n(0,t,\cup V_{i},\cup(N_{j})^{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-2Q\epsilon}\frac{1}{h}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

Then by (
27
) we have

\displaystyle a_{t+4\epsilon}(x,y)

\displaystyle\gtrsim e^{-2Q\epsilon}\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

Replacing
t
by
t-4\epsilon
, we have

(29)

\displaystyle a_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle\gtrsim e^{-2Q\epsilon}e^{-4\epsilon}\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

On the other hand, by Proposition
8.14
and Corollary
7.12
, for any
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}

(30)

\displaystyle e^{2Q\epsilon}\frac{1}{h}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}e^{2Q\epsilon}e^{-h(t_{0}^{i}+\epsilon^{2}+4\epsilon)}\mu_{p}((V_{i})^{-})\mu_{p}((N_{j})^{+})\frac{1}{h}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}n(0,t,V_{i},(N_{j})^{0})e^{-ht}e^{-h(\epsilon^{2}+4\epsilon)}.

Combining with (
28
) and Lemmas
8.15
,
8.16
, one has

\displaystyle a_{t-4\epsilon}(x,y)\lesssim Ce^{h^{\prime}(t-4\epsilon)}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Ce^{h(t-4\epsilon)}\cdot\mu_{p}((\cup_{j=n+1}^{\infty}N_{j})^{+})+Ce^{h(t-4\epsilon)}\cdot\mu_{p}((\cup_{j=m+1}^{\infty}V_{i})^{+})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{2Q\epsilon}e^{h(\epsilon^{2}+4\epsilon)}\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

Letting
m,n\to\infty
and replacing
t-4\epsilon
by
t
, we have

(31)

\displaystyle a_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle e^{2Q\epsilon}e^{h(\epsilon^{2}+4\epsilon)+4\epsilon}\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\int_{S_{x}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}M\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,p)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
in (
29
) and (
31
) and recalling that
p=x
, we get

a_{t}(x,y)\sim\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\cdot c(x,y)

where

c(x,y):=\int_{S_{x}X\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}\int_{S_{y}X\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,x)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

By Lemma
8.2
, in fact we have

c(x,y)=\int_{S_{x}X}\int_{S_{y}X}e^{-hb_{v^{-}}(\pi v,x)}d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(-v)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(w).

It follows that

b_{t}(x)=\int_{\mathcal{F}}a_{t}(x,y)d\text{Vol}(y)\sim\frac{1}{h}e^{ht}\int_{\mathcal{F}}c(x,y)d\text{Vol}(y)
by the dominated convergence Theorem. Indeed, by (
28
), Lemma
8.15
,
[
119
, Equation (10)]
and (
30
), there exist contants
B_{1},B_{2}>0
such that

e^{-ht}a_{t}(x,y)\leq B_{1}+B_{2}c(x,y),

and the right hand side is clearly integrable.

Define
c(x):=\int_{\mathcal{F}}c(x,y)d\text{Vol}(y)
, we get
b_{t}(x)\sim c(x)\frac{e^{ht}}{h}
.
Obviously,
c(\gamma x)=c(x)
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. So
c
descends to a function from
M
to
{\mathbb{R}}
, which still denoted by
c
.

It remains to prove the continuity of
c
. Let
y_{n}\to x\in X
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
d(x,y_{n})<\epsilon
. Then

b_{t-\epsilon}(x)\leq b_{t}(y_{n})\leq b_{t+\epsilon}(x).

We have

\displaystyle c(y_{n})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{b_{t}(y_{n})}{e^{ht}/h}\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{b_{t+\epsilon}(x)}{e^{ht}/h}=c(x)e^{h\epsilon}

and

\displaystyle c(y_{n})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{b_{t}(y_{n})}{e^{ht}/h}\geq\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{b_{t-\epsilon}(x)}{e^{ht}/h}=c(x)e^{-h\epsilon}.

Thus
\lim_{n\to\infty}c(y_{n})=c(x)
and
c
is continuous.
∎

9.
Margulis function and rigidity

9.1.
Properties of Margulis function

We will study the regularity properties of Margulis function and prove the integral formula for topological entropy in Theorem
F
in this section. Let
M
be a closed
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous asymptote,
X
the universal cover of
M
. Suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point.

It is easy to see that

(32)

s_{t}(x)/e^{ht}\sim c(x),

where
s_{t}(x)
is the spherical volume of the sphere
S(x,t)
around
x\in X
of radius
t>0
. Based on the Margulis function, we give an equivalent definition for the Patterson-Sullivan measure as a limit of spherical volume measure on spheres as follows.

Recall that
f_{x}:S_{x}X\to\partial X
,
f_{x}(v)=v^{+}
. Similarly, we define the canonical projection
f_{x}^{R}:S(x,R)\to\partial X
by
f_{x}^{R}(y)=v_{y}^{+}
where
v_{y}
is the unit normal vector of the sphere
S(x,R)
at
y
. For any continuous function
\varphi:\partial X\to{\mathbb{R}}
, define a measure on
{\partial X}
by

\nu_{x}^{R}(\varphi):=\frac{1}{e^{hR}}\int_{S(x,R)}\varphi\circ f_{x}^{R}(y)d\text{Vol}(y).

By (
32
),
\nu_{x}^{R}(\partial X)
is uniformly bounded from above and below, and hence there exist limit measures of
\nu_{x}^{R}
when
R\to\infty
. Take any limit measure
\nu_{x}
. By (
32
), we see that

\nu_{x}(\partial X)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{s_{R}(x)}{e^{hR}}=c(x).

Moreover, by definition one can check that

(1)

For any
p,\ q\in X
and
\nu_{p}
-a.e.
\xi\in{\partial X}
,

\frac{d\nu_{q}}{d\nu_{p}}(\xi)=e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(q,p)}.

(2)

\{\nu_{p}\}_{p\in X}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, i.e., for every Borel set
A\subset{\partial X}
and for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
we have

\nu_{\gamma p}(\gamma A)=\nu_{p}(A).

(2) is obvious. See
[
119
]
for the proof of (1). Thus
\{\nu_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a
h
-dimensional Busemann density.

By the following theorem,
\{\nu_{x}\}_{x\in X}
coincide with the Patterson-Sullivan measure
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in X}
up to a scalar constant. So we do not distinguish them in the following discussion.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
M=X/\Gamma
be a compact uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points. Then up to a multiplicative constant, the Busemann density is unique, i.e., the Patterson-Sullivan measure is the unique Busemann density.

Proof.

We sketch the idea of the proof, following closely
[
66
, Page 764-767]
.

Step 1:
Fix a reference point
p_{0}\in X
. For an arbitrary point
p\in X\setminus p_{0}
, let
\xi=c_{p_{0},p}(+\infty)
. For any positive number
\rho>R
(where
R
is the constant in Proposition
5.8
), we call the set

B^{\rho}_{r}(\xi):=pr_{p_{0}}(B(p,\rho)\cap S(p_{0},d(p,p_{0})))

a ball of radius
r=e^{-d(p,p_{0})}
at
\xi\in{\partial X}
. By the triangle inequality we know
pr_{p_{0}}B(p,\rho)\subseteq B^{2\rho}_{r}(\xi)
.
Then by Proposition
5.8
(3), for
\rho>2R
, there exists a constant number
b(\rho)>1
such that

\frac{1}{b(\rho)}e^{-h\cdot d(p,p_{0})}\leq\mu_{p_{0}}(B^{\rho}_{r}(\xi))\leq b(\rho)e^{-h\cdot d(p,p_{0})}.

Step 2:
For any subset
A\subseteq{\partial X}
we can define the
s
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
H^{s}(A)
of
A

and Hausdorff dimension
Hd(A)
of
A
as in
[
66
, Definition 4.5]
.
Then we can show that there exists a constant number
c>1
satisfying the following estimate

(33)

\frac{1}{c}\mu_{p_{0}}(A)\leq H^{h}(A)\leq c\cdot\mu_{p_{0}}(A),~\forall~\mbox{Borel~set}~A\subseteq{\partial X}.

Then the Hausdorff dimension of
{\partial X}
equals
h
. Moreover, each Busemann density is
h
-dimensional.

There is a fundamental difference in the proof of the above estimations in no conjugate points case. In the proof of a type of Vitali covering lemma
[
66
, Lemma 4.7]
, Knieper used the convexity property in nonpositive curvature, namely, the distance function of two geodesics is convex. The convexity is no longer valid for manifolds without conjugate points. Under assumption of uniform visibility, we can use quasi-convexity in Proposition
3.6
to replace convexity. Then we obtain the following

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
U\subset{\partial X}
be an open set. For fixed
\rho>0
and
\epsilon>0
there exist a constant
C(\rho)>0
and a countable covering of
U
by balls

B^{C(\rho)}_{r_{j}}(\xi_{j}),\quad 0<r_{j}\leq\epsilon

such that the balls
B^{\rho/3}_{r_{j}}(\xi_{j})
are subsets of
U
and pairwise disjoint.

Here
C(\rho)=A\rho+B+\rho
were
A,B
are constants from quasi-convexity. One can see that replacing
\rho
by
C(\rho)
does not affect the proof of (
33
) (
[
66
, Proof of Theorem 4.6]
).

Step 3:
Finally, we can prove the Patterson-Sullivan measure is ergodic with respect to the
\Gamma
-action on
{\partial X}
as
[
66
, Proposition 4.9]
. Then up to a multiplicative constant, there exists exactly one Busemann density which is realized by the Patterson-Sullivan measure, see
[
66
, Theorem 4.10]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
F
.

Recall that
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is the normalized Patterson-Sullivan measure. By the above discussion, particularly
\nu_{x}(\partial X)=c(x)
and
\frac{d\nu_{y}}{d\nu_{x}}(\xi)=e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}
, we have

\frac{d\bar{\mu}_{y}}{d\bar{\mu}_{x}}(\xi)=\frac{c(x)}{c(y)}e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}.

Then
c(y)=c(x)\int_{{\partial X}}e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}d\bar{\mu}_{x}(\xi).

By Lemma
3.8
,
y\mapsto b_{\xi}(y,x)
is
C^{2}
. Moreover,
\triangle_{y}b_{\xi}(y,x)=\text{div}(-X)=trU(y,\xi)
and it is continuous in
\xi
. It follows that
c
is
C^{1}
.

If
c
is constant, then
\int_{{\partial X}}e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}d\bar{\mu}_{x}(\xi)\equiv 1
. Taking Laplacian with respect to
y
on both sides, we have

\int_{{\partial X}}h(h-trU(y,\xi))e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}d\bar{\mu}_{x}(\xi)\equiv 0.

It follows that

h=\frac{\int_{{\partial X}}trU(y,\xi)e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}d\bar{\mu}_{x}(\xi)}{\int_{{\partial X}}e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)}d\bar{\mu}_{x}(\xi)}=\int_{{\partial X}}trU(y,\xi)d\bar{\mu}_{y}(\xi)

for any
y\in X
.
∎

9.2.
Rigidity in dimension two

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian surface of genus at least
2
without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundle in this subsection.

9.2.1.
Unique ergodicity of horocycle flow

A
horocycle flow
is a continuous flow
h_{s}
on
SM
whose orbits are horocycles, i.e., for
v\in SM
,
\{h_{s}v:s\in{\mathbb{R}}\}={\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v),
where
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
is the strong stable horocycle manifold of
v
in
SM
. Clotet
[
34
]
proved recently the unique ergodicity of the horocycle flow, see also
[
33
]
for the case of nonpositive curvature.

If
M
has constant negative curvature, Furstenberg
[
46
]
proved the unique ergodicity of the horocycle flow, which is extended to compact surfaces of variable negative curvature by Marcus
[
82
]
.
To apply Marcus’s method to our case, we need to define the horocycle flow using the so-called
Margulis parametrization
. Gelfert-Ruggiero
[
49
]
defined a quotient map
\chi:SM\to Y
by an equivalence relation “collapsing” each generalized strip to a single curve, which semiconjugates the geodesic flow on
SM
and a continuous flow
\psi^{t}
on
Y
. See Theorem
5.4
(i.e.,
[
81
, Theorem 1.1]
) for this result in a more general setting. Gelfert-Ruggiero
[
49
]
showed that
Y
is a topological
3
-manifold, and that the quotient flow is expansive, topologically mixing and has local product structure. In
[
34
]
, the the horocycle flow with Margulis parametrization
h_{s}^{M}
is defined on
Y
. Using Coudène’s theorem
[
35
]
, it is showed in
[
34
, Proposition 4.2]
that the horocycle flow
h_{s}^{M}
on
Y
is uniquely ergodic, and the unique invariant measure is
\chi_{*}m
, the projection of Bowen-Margulis measure
m
onto
Y
.

There is another natural parametrization of the horocycle flow on
SM
given by arc length of the horocycles, which clearly is well defined everywhere.
It is called the
Lebesgue parametrization
and the
Lebesgue horocycle flow
is denoted by
h_{s}^{L}
.
By constructing complete transversals to respective flows, it is showed in
[
34
, Theorem 5.8]
that there is a bijection between finite Borel measures invariant under
h_{s}^{M}
on
Z
and
h_{s}^{L}
on
SM
respectively.
Then
h_{s}^{L}
is also uniquely ergodic
[
34
, Theorem 5.10]
. We denote by
w^{s}
the unique probability measure invariant under the Lebesgue horocycle flow
h_{s}^{L}
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. Recall that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v):=

\displaystyle\{w\in SX:w^{-}=v^{-}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(v):=

\displaystyle\{w\in{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v):b_{v^{-}}(\pi w,\pi v)=0\}

are the weak and strong unstable horocycle manifold of
v
respectively.
Put
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(v):=-{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(-v)
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v):=-{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(-v)
.
Then
{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)
contains a relatively compact neighborhood
T
of
v
in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
, which is a
complete transversal
to the Lebesgue horocycle flow
h_{s}^{L}
in the sense of
[
34
, Defintion 5.5]
. So locally for a subset
E
in a regular neighborhood of
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
,

w^{s}(E)=\int_{{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}1_{E}(h_{s}^{L}(u))dsd\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)}(u)

where
1_{E}
is the characteristic function of
E
, and
\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)}
is some Borel measure on
T\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)
which is in fact independent of the parametrization of the horocycle flow. Note that
\chi
is a homeomorphism in a neighborhood of
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

On the other hand, the unique invariant measure for
h_{s}^{M}
on
Y
is the projection of Bowen-Margulis measure
m
, which can be expressed as

m(E)=\int_{\partial X}\int_{\partial X}\int_{\mathbb{R}}1_{E}(\xi,\eta,t)e^{h\cdot\beta_{p}(\xi,\eta)}dtd\mu_{p}(\xi)d\mu_{p}(\eta)

since
E
contains no generalized strips.
Consider the canonical projection

P=P_{v}:{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(v)\to{\partial X},\quad P(w)=w^{+},

then

\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{0u}(v)}(A)=\int\int_{\mathbb{R}}1_{A}(\phi^{t}u)dtd\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(v)}(u)

where
\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(v)}(B):=\int_{\partial X}1_{B}(P_{v}^{-1}\eta)e^{-hb_{\eta}(\pi v,p)}d\mu_{p}(\eta)
and
A,B
are in a regular neighborhood of
v\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

Thus
w^{s}
is locally equivalent to the measure
ds\times dt\times d\mu_{{\mathcal{F}}^{u}}
. If we disintegrate
w^{s}
along
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
foliation, the factor measure on a section
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(v)
is equivalent to
dsdt
, i.e., the Lebesgue measure
Vol
, and the conditional measures on the fiber
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}(v)
is equivalent to
P_{v}^{-1}\mu_{p}
.

9.2.2.
Uniqueness of harmonic measure

Let
\mathcal{G}
be any foliation on a compact Riemannian manifold
N
with
C^{2}
leaves. A probability measure
\nu
on
N
is called
harmonic
with respect to
\mathcal{G}
if
\int_{N}\triangle^{L}fd\nu=0
for any bounded measurable function
f
on
N
which is smooth in the leaf direction, where
\triangle^{L}
denotes the Laplacian in the leaf direction.

A
holonomy invariant measure
of the foliation
\mathcal{G}
is a family of measures defined on each transversal of
\mathcal{G}
, which is invariant under all the canonical homeomorphisms of the holonomy pseudogroup
[
94
]
. A measure is called
completely invariant
with respect to
\mathcal{G}
if it disintegrates to a constant function times the Lebesgue measure on the leaf, and the factor measure is a holonomy invariant measure on a transversal. By
[
47
]
, a completely invariant measure must be a harmonic measure. See
[
125
]
for more details on the ergodic properties of foliations.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed
C^{\infty}
uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and with continuous asymptote,
(X,\tilde{g})
the universal cover of
(M,g)
. Suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point. We can identify any weak stable manifold
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(v),v\in SX
with
X\times\{v^{+}\}
and endow
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}(v)
a Riemannian structure induced by
(X,\tilde{g})
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
is identified with the horosphere
H(v)\subset X
. Since
X
has continuous asymptote, by Proposition
3.7
every horosphere
H(v)
is a
C^{2}
submanifold in
X
(see also the proof of Theorem
F
). So
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
is endowed with a
C^{2}
Riemannian structure. Moreover, for any
\varphi\in C(X,{\mathbb{R}})
,

\triangle^{0s}\varphi=\bigtriangleup^{s}\varphi+\ddot{\varphi}-trU\dot{\varphi}

by
[
122
, Lemma 5.1]
, where
\bigtriangleup^{0s}
is the Laplacian along
X
(and hence along
{\mathcal{F}}^{0s}
-leaves) and
\bigtriangleup^{s}
is the Laplacian along the horospheres (and hence along
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
-leaves).

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian surface of genus at least
2
without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundle. Then there is precisely one harmonic probability measure with respect to the strong stable horocycle foliation.

Proof.

If
\dim M=2
, then the leaves of the strong stable horocycle foliation have polynomial volume growth. By
[
59
]
, any harmonic measure must be completely invariant. From the discussion in the previous subsection, we see that there is a unique completely invariant measure
w^{s}
. As a consequence,
w^{s}
is the unique harmonic measure.
∎

9.2.3.
Integral formulas for topological entropy

Recall that
M
is a closed Riemannian surface of genus at least
2
without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundle. Using the measure
w^{s}
we can establish some formulas for topological entropy
h
of the geodesic flow.

Let
B^{s}(v,R)
denote the ball centered at
v
of radius
R>0
inside
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}(v)
. In fact, it is just a curve. By the uniqueness of harmonic measure
w^{s}
, we have

Lemma 9.4
.

For any continuous
\varphi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\frac{1}{\text{Vol}(B^{s}(v,R))}\int_{B^{s}(v,R)}\varphi d\text{Vol}(y)\to\int_{SM}\varphi dw^{s}

as
R\to\infty
uniformly in
v\in SM
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
125
, Theorem 1.2]
and thus is omitted here.
∎

For continuous
\varphi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
, define
\varphi_{x}:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
\varphi_{x}(y)=\varphi(v(y))
where
v(y)\in SX
is the unique vector such that
c_{v(y)}(0)=y
and
c_{v(y)}(t)=x
for some
t\geq 0
.

Based on Lemma
9.4
, we get the following proposition.

Proposition 9.5
.

For any continuous
\varphi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\frac{1}{s_{R}(x)}\int_{S(x,R)}\varphi_{x}(y)d\text{Vol}(y)\to\int_{SM}\varphi dw^{s}

as
R\to\infty
uniformly in
x\in X
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the one before
[
125
, Proposition 3.1]
(see also
[
65
,
70
]
), and hence will be skipped. The basic idea here is that horospheres in
X
can be approximated by geodesic spheres.
∎

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian surface of genus at least
2
without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundle. Then

(1)

h=\int_{SM}trU(v)dw^{s}(v)
,

(2)

h^{2}=\int_{SM}-tr\dot{U}(v)+(trU(v))^{2}dw^{s}(v)
,

(3)

h^{3}=\int_{SM}tr\ddot{U}-3tr\dot{U}trU+(trU)^{3}dw^{s}
,

where
U(v)
and
trU(v)
are the second fundamental form and the mean curvature of the horocycle
H_{\pi v}(v^{+})
at
\pi v
.

Proof.

Consider the following function

G_{x}(R):=\frac{s_{R}(x)}{e^{hR}}=\frac{1}{e^{hR}}\int_{S(x,R)}d\text{Vol}(y).

Taking the derivatives, we have

\displaystyle G_{x}^{\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-hG_{x}(R)+\frac{1}{e^{hR}}\int_{S(x,R)}trU_{R}(y)d\text{Vol}(y),

\displaystyle G_{x}^{\prime\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-h^{2}G_{x}(R)-2hG_{x}^{\prime}(R)+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e^{hR}}\int_{S(x,R)}-tr\dot{U}_{R}(y)+(trU_{R}(y))^{2}d\text{Vol}(y),

\displaystyle G_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-h^{3}G_{x}(R)-3h^{2}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)-3hG_{x}^{\prime\prime}(R)+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e^{hR}}\int_{S(x,R)}tr\ddot{U}_{R}(y)-3tr\dot{U}_{R}(y)trU_{R}(y)+(trU_{R}(y))^{3}d\text{Vol}(y),

where
U_{R}(y)
and
trU_{R}(y)
are the second fundamental form and the mean curvature of
S(x,R)
at
y
.

Clearly,
trU_{R}(y)\to trU(v(y))
as
R\to\infty
uniformly. By Theorem
E
,

\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}(R)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{s_{R}(x)}{e^{hR}}=c(x).

Combining with Proposition
9.5
, we have

(34)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-hc(x)+c(x)\int_{SM}trUdw^{s},

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-h^{2}c(x)-2h\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)+c(x)\int_{SM}-tr\dot{U}+(trU)^{2}dw^{s},

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)=

\displaystyle-h^{3}c(x)-3h^{2}\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)-3h\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime\prime}(R)+

\displaystyle c(x)\int_{SM}tr\ddot{U}-3tr\dot{U}trU+(trU)^{3}dw^{s}.

Since
\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)
exists and
\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}(R)
is bounded, we have
\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime}(R)=0
. Similarly, considering the second and third derivative, we have

\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime\prime}(R)=\lim_{R\to\infty}G_{x}^{\prime\prime\prime}(R)=0.

Plugging in (
34
), we have

(1)

h=\int_{SM}trU(v)dw^{s}(v)
,

(2)

h^{2}=\int_{SM}-tr\dot{U}(v)+(trU(v))^{2}dw^{s}(v)
,

(3)

h^{3}=\int_{SM}tr\ddot{U}-3tr\dot{U}trU+(trU)^{3}dw^{s}
.

∎

9.2.4.
Rigidity

Recall that
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is a Borel measure on
S_{x}X
(hence descending to
S_{x}M
) induced by the Patterson-Sullivan measure
\mu_{x}
and let us assume that it is normalized, by a slight abuse of the notation. We have the following characterization of
w^{s}
.

Proposition 9.7
.

For any continuous
\varphi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

C\int_{SM}\varphi dw^{s}=\int_{M}c(x)\int_{S_{x}M}\varphi d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(v)d\text{Vol}(x)

where
C=\int_{M}c(x)d\text{Vol}(x)
.

Proof.

The idea is to show the right hand side is a harmonic measure up to a normalization. Then the proposition follows from Theorem
9.3
. The proof is completely parallel to that of
[
125
, Proposition 4.1]
(see also
[
124
,
70
]
), and hence is omitted.
∎

Proof of Theorem
G
.

By Theorem
9.6
,

h^{2}=\int_{SM}-tr\dot{U}(v)+(trU(v))^{2}dw^{s}(v).

Recall the Ricatti equation in dimension two:

-\dot{U}+U^{2}+K=0

where
K
is the Gaussian curvature.
Since now
U
is just a real number and hence
tr(U^{2})=(trU)^{2}
. Using Proposition
9.7
and Gauss-Bonnet formula we have

\displaystyle h^{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{SM}-Kdw^{s}=\frac{1}{C}\int_{M}-c(x)K(x)d\text{Vol}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int-Kd\text{Vol}/\text{Vol}(M)=-2\pi E/\text{Vol}(M),

where
E
is the Euler characteristic of the surface
M
. By Katok’s result
[
62
, Theorem B]
,

h^{2}=-2\pi E/\text{Vol}(M)
if and only if
M
has constant negative curvature.
∎

9.3.
Flip invariance of the Patterson-Sullivan measure

Let
M
be a closed
C^{\infty}
Riemannian manifold without conjugate points and with continuous Green bundle. Suppose that the geodesic flow has a hyperbolic periodic point. We already see that
y\mapsto b_{\xi}(y,x)
is
C^{2}
in Lemma
3.8
.

For each
x\in X
, denote by
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
both the Borel probability measure on
S_{x}X
and
\partial X
given by the normalized Patterson-Sullivan measure.
Define a measure
w^{s}
by

C\int_{SM}\varphi dw^{s}:=\int_{M}c(x)\int_{S_{x}M}\varphi d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(v)d\text{Vol}(x)

for any continuous
\varphi:SM\to{\mathbb{R}}
, where
C=\int_{M}c(x)d\text{Vol}(x)
.

In view of Proposition
9.7
,
w^{s}
is a harmonic measure associated to the strong stable foliation
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
, though the uniqueness of harmonic measure is unknown in general. We do not need the uniqueness of harmonic measure in this subsection.

Proposition 9.8
.

For
\varphi\in C^{1}(SM)
, one has

\int_{SM}\dot{\varphi}+(h-trU)\varphi dw^{s}=0.

Proof.

Define a vector field on
M
by

Y(y):=\int_{S_{y}M}\varphi X(v)d\tilde{\mu}_{y}(v)=\int_{S_{x}M}\varphi X(v)e^{-hb_{v}(y)}d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(v)

where
X
is the geodesic spray. Since
\triangledown b_{v}=-X
and
divX=-trU
, one has

\displaystyle div|_{y=x}Y

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{x}M}div|_{y=x}\varphi X(v)e^{-hb_{v}(y)}d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{x}M}\dot{\varphi}+(h-trU)\varphi d\tilde{\mu}_{x}.

Integrating with respect to
Vol
on
M
and using Green’s formula, we have
\int_{SM}\dot{\varphi}+(h-trU)\varphi dw^{s}=0.

∎

Proposition 9.9
.

If
w^{s}
is
\phi^{t}
-invariant, then
M
is locally symmetric.

Proof.

If
w^{s}
is
\phi^{t}
-invariant, by Proposition
9.8
, we have

\int_{SM}(h-trU)\varphi dw^{s}=0

for all
\varphi\in C^{1}(SM,{\mathbb{R}})
. It follows that
trU\equiv h
. Recall that
y\mapsto b_{\xi}(y,x)
is
C^{2}
. So
M
is asymptotically harmonic. By
[
127
, Theorem 1.2]
,
M
is locally symmetric, since
X
has purely exponential volume growth.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

If for all
x\in M
,
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is flip invariant, then the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
coincides with the Liouville measure
Leb
on
SM
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
124
, Proposition 2.1]
works just as well here. We remark that by uniform visibility, for every pair
\xi\neq\eta\in{\partial X}
, there exists a geodesic
c_{\xi,\eta}
connecting
\xi
and
\eta
.
∎

Lemma 9.11
.

If for all
x\in M
,
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is flip invariant, then the Margulis function
c(x)
is constant.

Proof.

Any
\varphi\in C^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
can be lifted to a function on
SM
which we still denote by
\varphi
. Recall that we have identified each weak stable manifold with
X
, so the Laplacian along the weak stable foliation
\bigtriangleup^{0s}
is exactly the Laplacian
\triangle
along
X
. By
[
122
, Lemma 5.1]
,
\triangle^{0s}\varphi=\bigtriangleup^{s}\varphi+\ddot{\varphi}-trU\dot{\varphi}
.
Then by definition of
w^{s}
and Proposition
9.8
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\bigtriangleup\varphi c(x)d\text{Leb}=C\int\bigtriangleup\varphi dw^{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\int_{SM}(\bigtriangleup^{s}\varphi+\ddot{\varphi}-trU\dot{\varphi})dw^{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\left(\int_{SM}\bigtriangleup^{s}\varphi dw^{s}+\int_{SM}\ddot{\varphi}+(h-trU)\dot{\varphi}dw^{s}-\int_{SM}h\dot{\varphi}dw^{s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-h\int_{M}c(x)d\text{Leb}(x)\int\dot{\varphi}(x,\xi)d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(\xi).

Since
d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(\xi)=d\tilde{\mu}_{x}(-\xi)
and
\dot{\varphi}(x,\xi)=-\dot{\varphi}(x,-\xi)
, we have

\int_{M}\bigtriangleup\varphi c(x)d\text{Leb}=0

for any
\varphi\in C^{2}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
. So
c(x)
must be constant.
∎

Proof of Theorem
H
.

By the construction, the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
is flip invariant. By the flip invariance of the partition
\{S_{x}M\}_{x\in M}
and the uniqueness of conditional measures, we see that
\bar{\mu}_{x}
is flip invariant for
m\text{-a.e.}\ x\in M
. It follows that the normalized Patterson-Sullivan measures
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is flip invariant for
m\text{-a.e.}\ x\in M
.

We claim that for all
x\in M
,
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
is flip invariant. Indeed, note that for fixed
x
, the density

\frac{d\tilde{\mu}_{y}}{d\tilde{\mu}_{x}}(\xi)=\frac{c(x)}{c(y)}e^{-h\cdot b_{\xi}(y,x)},

is uniformly continuous in
y
. For each continuous function
\varphi:\partial X\to{\mathbb{R}}
, its geodesic reflection with respect to
z\in X
is defined by
\varphi_{z}(\xi):=\varphi(c_{z,\xi}(-\infty))
. Let
x_{k},x\in X
and
x_{k}\to x
as
k\to\infty
. Then by the above continuity,

\int_{\partial X}\varphi d\tilde{\mu}_{x}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\partial X}\varphi d\tilde{\mu}_{x_{k}}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\partial X}\varphi_{x_{k}}d\tilde{\mu}_{x_{k}}=\int_{\partial X}\varphi_{x}d\tilde{\mu}_{x}.

The claim follows.

By Lemma
9.10
, the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
coincides with the Liouville measure, and thus
m
projects to the Riemannian volume on
M
. By assumption, the conditional measures
\bar{\mu}_{x}
coincides with
\tilde{\mu}_{x}
. Moreover, by Lemma
9.11
,
c(x)
is constant. Consequently, we see from definition that
w^{s}
coincides with the Bowen-Margulis measure
m
, and hence it is
\phi^{t}
-invariant. By Proposition
9.9
,
M
is locally symmetric.
∎

10.
The Hopf-Tsuji-Sullivan dichotomy

Let
X
be a simply connected smooth uniform visibility manifold without conjugate points and
\Gamma\subseteq Is(X)
a discrete group. The
geometric limit set
of
\Gamma
is defined as
L_{\Gamma}:=\overline{\Gamma x}\cap{\partial X}
where
x\in X
is an arbitrary point. Suppose that
\Gamma\subseteq Is(X)
is a
non-elementary
discrete group, i.e., the cardinality of
L_{\Gamma}
is infinity. We also suppose that
\Gamma
contains an
expansive isometry

h\in\Gamma
, i.e.,
h
has an axis
c
with
\dot{c}(0)\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
. We emphasize that in this section
M=X/\Gamma
is not necessarily compact.

10.1.
The generalized shadow lemma

For
r>0,c>0
and
x,y\in X
we set

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{+}(x,y):=\{\xi\in{\partial X}:\exists z\in B(x,r)\text{\ such that\ }c_{z,\xi}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})\cap B(y,c)\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(x,y):=\{\xi\in{\partial X}:\forall z\in B(x,r)\text{\ we have \ }c_{z,\xi}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})\cap B(y,c)\neq\emptyset\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{r,c}(x,y):=\{(\xi,\eta)\in{\partial X}\times{\partial X}:\exists x^{\prime}\in B(x,r),\exists y^{\prime}\in B(y,c)\text{\ such that\ }

\displaystyle c_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(-\infty)=\xi,\ c_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}(+\infty)=\eta\}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(x,y)\subset pr_{x}(B(y,c))\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{+}(x,y),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{r,c}(x,y)\supset\{(\xi,\eta)\in\partial^{2}X:\eta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(x,y),\ \xi\in pr_{\eta}(B(x,r)\}.

As
\Gamma
is non-elementary, there exists one element (actually an infinite number) in
\Gamma
not commuting with
h
so that conjugation by such an element gives another expansive isometry whose fixed points in
{\partial X}
are disjoint from those of
h
. By a parallel argument in the proof of
[
6
, Proposition 2.8]
, the geometric limit set of
\Gamma
is minimal, i.e.
L_{\Gamma}=\overline{\Gamma\cdot\xi}
for any
\xi\in L_{\Gamma}
. This implies that for any open subset
O\subset{\partial X}
with
O\cap L_{\Gamma}\neq\emptyset
there exists a finite set
\Lambda\subset\Gamma
depending on
O
such that

L_{\Gamma}\subset\bigcup_{\beta\in\Lambda}\beta O.

See the proof of
[
73
, Lemma 2.8]
using Proposition
7.9
which works as well here.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
h\in\Gamma
be the expansive isometry from above,
r_{0}>0
, and
U,V\subset\overline{X}
the neighborhoods of
h^{-}
and
h^{+}
provided by Proposition
7.7
for
r_{0}
. Then there
exists a finite set
\Lambda\subset\Gamma
such that the following holds:
For any
c>0
there exists
R>1
large enough such that if
\gamma\in\Gamma
satisfies
d(o,\gamma o)>R
, then for some
\beta\in\Lambda
we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{r,c}(o,\beta\gamma o)\cap(U\times V)\supset(U\cap{\partial X})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(o,\beta\gamma o).

Proof.

Th proof uses the cone topology of
{\partial X}
and is analogous to that of
[
76
, Proposition 1]
. The slight difference here is that
h
is an expansive isometry and hence has width zero.
∎

Since
\Gamma
is non-elementary, let
g\in\Gamma
be another expansive isometry with fixed points in
{\partial X}
distinct from those of
h
. The following result roughly demonstrates that
{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(y,o)
contains an open neighborhood and hence is rather big, uniformly with respect to
y\in X
for a suitable large
c
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Given
r>0
there exist an open neighborhood
O\subset{\partial X}
of
h^{+}
,
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
c_{0}>0
such that for all
y\in X
with
d(y,o)>r+c_{0}
,

h^{M}(O)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(y,o),\text{\ or \ }g^{M}h^{M}(O)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(y,o).

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
76
, Proposition 2]
, which essentially relies on Propositions
7.7
and
7.9
.
∎

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
x\in X
and
A\subset L_{\Gamma}
a
\Gamma
-invariant Borel set. Then
\mu_{x}(A)>0
implies
\mu_{x}(O\cap A)>0
for any open set
O\subset{\partial X}
with
O\cap L_{\Gamma}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
76
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

Proposition 10.4
.

For any
r>0
there exists a constant
c_{0}\geq r
with the following property: If
c\geq c_{0}
there exists a constant
D=D(c)>1
such that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
d(o,\gamma o)>2c
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D}e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}\leq\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(o,\gamma o))

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{o}(pr_{o}B(\gamma o,c))

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(o,\gamma o))\leq De^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}.

Proof.

Fix
r>0
. According to Lemma
10.2
, there exist open neighborhood
O\subset{\partial X}
of
h^{+}
and
c_{0}\geq r
, such that for any
c\geq c_{0}
, any
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
d(\gamma o,o)>r+c
, there exists
\beta\in\{h^{M},g^{M}h^{M}\}
such that

\beta O\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c_{0}}^{-}(\gamma o,o)\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(\gamma o,o).

Let
q:=\min\{\mu_{o}(h^{M}O),\mu_{o}(g^{M}h^{M}O)\}
, which is positive by Lemma
10.3
. Thus for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
d(o,\gamma o)>2c
we have

\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(\gamma o,o))\geq\mu_{o}(\beta O)\geq q>0

and

\displaystyle\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(o,\gamma o))

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma^{-1}o}({\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(\gamma^{-1}o,o))

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{r,c}^{-}(\gamma^{-1}o,o))\geq e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}q.

The last inequality in the proposition actually holds for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. First, let us notice the following fact: If
x,y\in X
and
\eta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(x,y)
, we have

b_{\eta}(x,y)\geq d(x,y)-4c.

Hence if
d(o,\gamma o)>2c
,

\displaystyle\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(o,\gamma o))

\displaystyle=\mu_{\gamma^{-1}o}({\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(\gamma^{-1}o,o))

\displaystyle\leq e^{4c\delta_{\Gamma}}e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}\mu_{o}({\partial X}).

If
d(o,\gamma o)\leq 2c
, then
{\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(o,\gamma o)={\partial X}
and hence

\displaystyle\mu_{o}({\mathcal{O}}_{c,c}^{+}(o,\gamma o))=\mu_{o}({\partial X})\leq e^{2c\delta_{\Gamma}}e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}\mu_{o}({\partial X}).

Let
D=D(c):=\max\{e^{4c\delta_{\Gamma}}\mu_{o}({\partial X}),1/q\}
and we are done.
∎

10.2.
Properties of the radial limit set

We define an important subset of geometric limit set. For
c>0
and
R\gg 1
, set

\displaystyle L_{\Gamma}(c,R):=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma,d(o,\gamma o)>R}pr_{o}(B(\gamma o,c)),

\displaystyle L_{\Gamma}(c):=

\displaystyle\bigcap_{R>0}L_{\Gamma}(c,R),

\displaystyle L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma}:=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{c>0}L_{\Gamma}(c).

We call
L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma}
the
radial limit set
of
\Gamma
, and it is independent of the choice of the origin
o\in X
.

Lemma 10.5
.

(
[
78
, Thereom 5.5]
)
If
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}e^{-\delta_{\Gamma}d(o,\gamma o)}
converges, then

\mu_{o}(L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma})=0.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
78
, Thereom 5.5]
or the proof of
[
76
, Lemma 5.1]
for nonpositive curvature case.
∎

Proposition 10.6
.

(
[
78
, Thereom 5.6]
)
If
A\subset L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma}
is a
\Gamma
-invariant Borel subset of
L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma}
, then
\mu_{o}(A)=0
or
\mu_{o}(A)=\mu_{o}({\partial X})
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
78
, Thereom 5.6]
. The analogous proof for nonpositive curvature is given in
[
76
, Proposition 4]
.
∎

Proposition 10.7
.

(
[
78
, Thereom 5.4]
)
A radial limit point cannot be a point mass for
m_{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
78
, Thereom 5.4]
. In nonpositive curvature, the proof is given in
[
76
, Proposition 5]
.
∎

10.3.
Dynamical properties of the geodesic flow

The following relation between conservative point and radial limit point can be deduced from the definition.

Lemma 10.8
.

(
[
78
, Thereom 6.2]
)

{\underline{v}}\in SM
is a conservative point, if and only if
c_{v}(+\infty)\in L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma}
for some (hence any) lift
v\in SX
.

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
M=X/\Gamma
be a complete visibility manifold with no conjugate points where
\Gamma
is a non-elementary discrete subgroup containing an expansive isometry, and
m_{\Gamma}
a Bowen-Margulis measure defined on
SM
. We have the following dichotomy.

(1)

\mu_{o}(L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma})=\mu_{o}({\partial X})
, and the geodesic flow is completely conservative with respect t
m_{\Gamma}
; OR

(2)

\mu_{o}(L^{\text{rad}}_{\Gamma})=0
